Bordism and resolution of singularities with applications to Gromov-Witten theory Thanks: The first and second authors were respectively supported by NSF grants DMS-2103805 and DMS-2404843
Mohammed Abouzaid, Shaoyun Bai

Abstract
. We adapt algorithms for resolving the singularities of complex algebraic varieties to prove that the natural map of homology theories from complex bordism to the bordism theory of complex derived orbifolds splits. In equivariant stable homotopy theory, our techniques yield a splitting of homology theories for the map from bordism to the equivariant bordism theory of a finite group Γ \Gamma , given by assigning to a manifold its product with Γ \Gamma . In symplectic topology, and using recent work of Abouzaid-McLean-Smith and Hirschi-Swaminathan, we conclude that one can define complex cobordism-valued Gromov-Witten invariant for arbitrary (closed) symplectic manifolds. We apply our results to constrain the topology of the space of Hamiltonian fibrations over S 2 S^{2} . The methods we develop apply to normally complex orbifolds, and will hence lead to applications in symplectic topology that rely on moduli spaces of holomorphic curves with Lagrangian boundary conditions.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Applications: equivariant stable homotopy theory

While our initial motivation for this work arose from symplectic topology (see Section
1.2
below), we begin with a discussion of its consequences for equivariant stable homotopy, as they may be familiar to a wider set of potential readers. The reader who is interested largely in the study of (derived) orbifolds and in the methods we use to study them may skip instead to Section
1.3
.

Associated to each finite group
\Gamma
, and each theory of bordism (oriented, complex, spin, …) is an equivariant homology theory which assigns to a topological space
X
, equipped with a
\Gamma
-action, the graded abelian group of bordism classes of
\Gamma
-manifolds equipped with a
\Gamma
-equivariant map to
X
. This theory is naturally a module over the bordism ring of manifolds, arising from taking products. In the case where
X
is a point, the first extensive investigation of these groups goes back to Conner and Floyd
[
CF64
]
. In this paper, we specialize our discussion to complex bordism, which we denote by
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*}(X)
, whose elements are represented by bordism classes of
\Gamma
-manifolds equipped with an almost complex structure on the direct sum of the tangent bundle with
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
(with trivial
\Gamma
-action).

Remark 1.1
.

We formulate all our results as statements about homology theories because this will not take us too far afield from the other goals of the paper which are described in the remainder of this introduction. There are two ways to refine the results to statements about spectra: one can either pass to the dual cohomology theories, following the method described in Section
9.2.2
, or one can directly construct representing bordism spectra by extending our constructions from manifolds with boundary to those with corners
[
Qui95
,
AB24
]
.

Remark 1.2
.

In homotopy theoretic literature, there are analogous groups called
geometric bordism groups
, denoted by
\bm{mu}^{\Gamma}_{*}
, that can be described using Thom spaces on equivariant Grassmanians
[
Was69
]
1
1

1

Recently, Schwede has been advocating the use of the notation
\bm{mu}^{\Gamma}_{*}(X)
for these groups
[
Sch18
]
.
. The geometric description of these groups is in terms of bordism classes of
\Gamma
-manifolds equipped with an almost complex structure on the direct sum of the tangent bundle with
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\oplus V
, where
V
is a
complex representation
of
\Gamma
, so that there is a natural morphism of homology theories
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*}\to\bm{mu}^{\Gamma}_{*}
. It follows from Theorem
1.7
below that this map of equivariant homology theories splits.

The product of a stably complex manifold
M
with
\Gamma
is tautologically equipped with a
\Gamma
-invariant stable complex structure. Assuming that
X
is a space equipped with the trivial
\Gamma
-action, this construction yields a map
\Omega^{U}_{*}(X)\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*}(X)
, which can be readily seen to be multiplicative, and functorial with respect to maps of topological spaces. In particular, this map is naturally a map of modules over the (ordinary) bordism ring
\Omega^{U}_{*}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

For each finite group
\Gamma
, the map of homology theories
\Omega^{U}\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma}
splits as a map of
\Omega^{U}_{*}
-modules.

While the proof, based on the results of Sections
3
and
4
, will be provided in Section
5.3
, the basic idea is the following: the map in the other direction assigns to any
\Gamma
-manifold a resolution of the singularities of its quotient orbifold.

Remark 1.4
.

There is an altogether different map
\Omega^{U}\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma}
obtained by equipping a manifold with the trivial
\Gamma
action, and which has an obvious splitting given by forgetting the group action. These two maps are the special cases of a construction that assigns to each finite
\Gamma
set
S
the map
\Omega^{U}\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma}
which takes the product of a manifold with
S
. As we indicate in Remark
5.5
, our results produce a splitting of the map from a direct sum of copies of
\Omega^{U}
indexed by irreducible
\Gamma
-sets (i.e. conjugacy classes of subgroups) to
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}
.

Remark 1.5
.

The computation of the homology theories
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}
has been largely open since their introduction by Conner and Floyd
[
CF64
]
. For the theory
\bm{mu}^{\Gamma}
, the bordism groups of a point, for cyclic groups, were computed in
[
KY83
]
for cyclic groups (c.f. the survey
[
May96
]
for a discussion of more general abelian groups).

Remark 1.6
.

The naive generalizations of this result, to oriented bordism and to the Lie group setting, both fail. In the oriented bordism case, this is due to Rosenzweig
[
Ros72
]
who proved that every torsion element of the oriented bordism group lies in the kernel of the transfer map to the bordism group of oriented manifolds with orientation preserving involutions (the proof is completely explicit, relying on Anderson’s presentation of the torsion subgroup of the oriented bordism groups in terms of projective bundles
[
And66
]
). On the other hand, for Lie groups, one immediately sees that the circle bounds equivariantly. We can, however, prove an analogue of the above result for equivariant bordism of
almost-free
manifolds (i.e. those with the property that all stabilizer groups are finite).

Most of the work on equivariant bordism theories has been concerned with the following more sophisticated notion
[
tD70
]
: assign to a complex
\Gamma
-representation
V
and a space
X
the bordism group
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*+\dim V}(X\times V)
, generated by
\Gamma
-manifolds equipped with a proper equivariant map to
X\times V
, and define
d\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*}(X)
to be the colimit of these groups with respect to the maps

(1.1)

\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*+\dim V}(X\times V)\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*+\dim W}(X\times W),

associated to an inclusion
V\subset W
of complex
\Gamma
-representations, which are given by taking the product with a complementary subspace to
V
. This theory is usually called
homotopical bordism
, and following Schwede, the corresponding groups are denoted in some of the recent literature in equivariant homotopy theory by a variant of the symbol
\bm{MU}_{*}^{\Gamma}(X)
.

Theorem 1.7
.

For each finite group
\Gamma
, the map of equivariant homology theories
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}\to d\Omega^{U,\Gamma}
splits as a map of
\Omega^{U}_{*}
modules.

Remark 1.8
.

We suspect that the map we construct fails to be compatible with the module structure over the larger bordism ring
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}_{*}
. We could, however, prove compatiblity with external multiplication, i.e. the maps generalising the
\Omega^{U}_{*}
module map

(1.2)

\Omega^{U,\Gamma_{1}}\times\Omega^{U,\Gamma_{2}}\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}}.

The internal multiplication arises from the composition of the external multiplication with the restriction map along the diagonal of
\Gamma\times\Gamma
, and there is no reason for the splitting we construct to be compatible with restriction to subgroups.

The proof of this result is given in Section
8.3
, building upon the results of Sections
6
and
7
. The basic idea is the following: the difference between geometric and homotopical bordism measures the failure of equivariant transversality. By considering higher jets, one may nonetheless formulate a notion of
genericity
for equivariant maps which are modelled after holomorphic maps. Generic fibers of equivariant maps have singularities that are prescribed by representation theoretic data, and that are modelled by algebraic varieties. The splitting is provided by a resolution of singularities of such zero loci.

Remark 1.9
.

In the case of abelian groups, the map of coefficients
\bm{mu}^{\Gamma}_{*}\to\bm{MU}_{*}^{\Gamma}
is known to be injective
[
Löf73
,
tD70
]
, but we are not aware of any result either at the level of homology theories, or for general groups.

Our proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.7
are uniform for all groups
\Gamma
. Instead of using the language of global homology theories (c.f.
[
Sch18
]
), we give explicit statements, formulating the compatibility of the second result with two maps on bordism groups associated to a group homomorphism
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
: the first assigns to a
\Gamma
-manifold
M
the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-manifold
M\times_{\Gamma}\Gamma^{\prime}
, yielding, for each
\Gamma
-space
X
, a map from the
\Gamma
-equivariant bordism of
X
to the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant bordism of
X\times_{\Gamma}\Gamma^{\prime}
, while the second pulls back the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-action on a manifold to
\Gamma
, yielding, for each
\Gamma^{\prime}
-space
Y
, a map from
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant bordism to
\Gamma
-equivariant bordism:

Theorem 1.10
.

There is a distinguished choice of splitting in Theorem
1.7
, so that the following holds for a group homomorphism
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
:

(1)

If the homomorphism is injective, and
(X,A)
is a pair of
\Gamma
-spaces, the following diagram commutes:

(1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\Omega^{U,\Gamma}(X,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime}}(X\times_{\Gamma}\Gamma^{\prime},A\times_{\Gamma}\Gamma^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{U,\Gamma}(X,A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime}}(X\times_{\Gamma}\Gamma^{\prime},A\times_{\Gamma}\Gamma^{\prime}).}

(2)

If the homomorphism is surjective, and
(Y,B)
is a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-space, the following diagram commutes:

(1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime}}(Y,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\Omega^{U,\Gamma}(Y,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime}}(Y,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{U,\Gamma}(Y,B).}

Returning to Theorem
1.3
, we begin with the result that asserts the existence of a structure map which is not part of the usual axioms for equivariant homology theories:

Theorem 1.11
.

Given an surjective group homomorphism
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
, there is a natural transformation of homology theories

(1.5)

\Omega^{U,\Gamma}\Rightarrow\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime}}

on the category of pairs of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-spaces. This natural transformation is functorial with respect to group homomorphisms in the sense that, given a surjection
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, and a pair
(Y,B)
of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
-spaces, the following diagram commutes:

(1.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{U,\Gamma}(Y,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime}}(Y,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}(Y,B).}

As one might guess from the discussion following Theorem
1.3
, the map assigns to a
\Gamma
-manifold a resolution of the singularties of its quotient by the kernel of the homomorphism. The naturality of the resolution algorithm implies that the result carries a residual
\Gamma^{\prime}
-action. Our methods do not seem well-adapted to extend this result to homotopical bordism.

The relationship of the above statements to Theorem
1.3
, as well as to more familiar maps in equivariant homotopy theory, is most easily formulated if we assume that the surjection splits. In that case, given a pair
(Y,B)
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-spaces, we consider the composition:

(1.7)

\Omega^{U,\Gamma^{\prime}}(Y,B)\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma}(Y\times_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\Gamma,B\times_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\Gamma)\to\Omega^{U,\Gamma}(Y,B),

where the first map is induced by the injection
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
, and the second by the surjection
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
. This defines a natural transformation of functors on the category of such pairs.

Theorem 1.12
.

The composition of the natural transformations of Equations (
1.7
) and (
1.5
) is the identity on the category of pairs of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-spaces.

Remark 1.13
.

There has been some recent work showing that the coefficients of both geometric and homotopical bordism are non-trivial in odd degree
[
Sam22
,
Kri23
,
ASSU23
]
. The examples that they study arise from free actions, which can be identified with
\Omega^{U}_{*}(B\Gamma)
; the composition of the splitting constructed in Theorem
1.3
(when evaluated on a point) with the natural map from free bordism to equivariant bordism agrees with the map induced by the projection from
B\Gamma
to the point. In this sense, Theorem
1.3
can be seen as largely orthogonal to the existing literature, as it concerns the relationship between the free and non-free part of equivariant bordism.

On the other hand, Theorem
1.7
implies that a counterexample to the evenness conjecture for
\Omega^{U,\Gamma}
directly yields a counterexample for homotopical bordism.

1.2.
Applications: enumerative geometry and symplectic topology

We now turn attention to our initial motivation for this work. The reader who is interested in understanding the methods of this paper may entirely skip this section, and proceed to Section
1.3
, where we discuss bordism of orbifolds and derived orbifolds.

Classically, Gromov–Witten invariants of complex algebraic varieties or symplectic manifolds are constructed from virtual fundamental classes of moduli spaces of stable maps. Such a class lives in the rational Chow group or the rational homology group of the corresponding moduli space. It has been speculated since the beginning of Gromov–Witten theory
[
Kon95
, Section 1.5]
that it should be possible to formulate variants of these virtual fundamental classes taking value in complex cobordism. On the symplectic side, early work in the subject
[
McD87
]
has already showcased the use of cobordism classes of moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic maps to distinguish symplectic structures.

The results of this paper lead to complex bordism valued Gromov–Witten invariants for general symplectic manifolds which can be formulated as follows: given a closed symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
, let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
be the moduli space of stable genus
g
maps with
k
marked points which represent a homology class
A
on
X
, and which are pseudo-holomorphic with respect to an
\omega
-tamed almost complex structure
J
. The space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
comes with a continuous map

(1.8)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}\times X^{k},

which is the product of the stabilization map to the (coarse space of) Deligne–Mumford space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}
and the evaluation map at the
k
marked points.

Whenever all points in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
are regular, and have trivial isotropy, this moduli space is a closed manifold, and while it does not appear to admit a canonical smooth structure in general, there is an associated well-defined complex cobordism class, arising from a stable isomorphism between the tangent space and the kernel of the linearised
\bar{\partial}
operator, and the fact that this operator is complex linear up to terms of order
0
. In this (extremely) limited context, which already fails, for example, when considering genus-
0
Gromov–Witten theory with target
X=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, one obtains a Gromov–Witten invariant valued in the complex cobordism group of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}\times X^{k}
, which is defined as the image of this class under evaluation. The following result asserts that we may generalize this to all symplectic manifolds:

Theorem 1.14
.

For any closed symplectic manifold
X
, any pair
(g,k)
of non-negative integers, and any integral second homology class
A
on
X
, there is a complex cobordism class on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}\times X^{k}
which is associated to the moduli space of genus
g
stable maps with
k
marked points in homology class
A
. This class is independent of the choice of almost complex structure
J
, and agrees with the class defined by

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
whenever it is a transversely cut out manifold.

Note that due to the failure of transversality and the nontriviality of automorphisms of stable maps,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
is only a compact orbispace in general. The content of the above theorem is that we can construct a stably complex
manifold
from a derived orbifold
/global Kuranishi chart presentation of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
such that the resulting bordism class is independent of auxiliary choices. This is achieved by developing recent advances on
normally complex polynomial perturbations

[
BX22b
]
and adapting various methods of
resolution of singularities
in characteristic
0
to our context. We refer the reader to Theorem
9.3
for a more precise statement, and we will discuss our construction in later part of the introduction.

Remark 1.15
.

Coates–Givental
[
CG06
]
proposed a definition of rational quantum cobordisms by studying the Chern–Dold character of the virtual tangent bundle of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
and integrating it against the rational virtual fundamental class in the spirit of Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch theorem. In contrast, our definition is integral, and directly constructs stably complex manifolds.

As a proof of principle, we use our construction to establish a family version of the homological splitting theorem from
[
AMS21
]
. For a closed symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
, denote by
\Omega\mathrm{Ham}(M,\omega)
the space of loops of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms which are based at the identity. To each point in
\Omega\mathrm{Ham}(M,\omega)
, we have an associated sweep-out map
S^{1}\times X\to X
, which we can formulate as a map of based spaces

(1.9)

\displaystyle\Omega\mathrm{Ham}(X,\omega)\wedge S^{1}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Aut}(X),

where
\mathrm{Aut}
is the space of self-homotopy equivalences, with basepoint the identity.
Passing to spectra, we obtain a map

(1.10)

\Omega\mathrm{Ham}(X,\omega)\wedge S^{1}\wedge{\mathbb{S}}\to F(X_{+}\wedge{\mathbb{S}},X_{+}\wedge{\mathbb{S}}).

where the notation
F
stands for the mapping spectrum, taking value in the component of self-homotopy equivalences.
Our main result is formulated by further passing to complex cobordism:

Theorem 1.16
.

The map

(1.11)

\Omega\mathrm{Ham}(X,\omega)\wedge S^{1}\to F(X_{+}\wedge{\mathbb{S}},X_{+}\wedge MU)

is null-homotopic.

Composing this result with the inclusion of any point in
\Omega\mathrm{Ham}(X,\omega)
recovers
[
AMS21
, Theorem 1.3]
as a special case. However, unlike
loc. cit.
, our proof does not depend on any chromatic homotopy theory. In particular, while it would not be too difficult to obtain such a null-homotopy
after
p
-completion
for each prime
p
from the techniques of
[
AMS21
]
, we do not know how to produce a compatible collection of null-homotopies from the chromatic construction.

1.3.
Results: bordism for orbispaces

The key notions that give rise to the results discussed in Sections
1.1
and
1.2
are best formulated in the language of bordism theories for orbispaces.

Following Pardon
[
Par21
]
, we consider the model
\operatorname{RepOrbTop}
for the category of
orbispaces
, modelled by orbi-CW complexes, whose objects are obtained by gluing products of discs with the orbifolds associated to
\operatorname{pt}/\Gamma
, for finite groups
\Gamma
, along maps which are representable, i.e. induce injections of stabilizer groups. The morphisms in
\operatorname{RepOrbTop}
are also assumed to be representable. The restriction to representable morphisms should be considered as a mild assumption, as discussed in the introduction to
[
Par21
]
.

The classical geometric approach to defining bordism as a homology theory (cf.
[
Ati61
]
) can be imitated for derived orbifolds (cf.
[
Joy07
,
Par21
]
), so that one obtains a bordism homology theory. We specifically consider the stably complex orbifold bordism group
\Omega^{U}_{*}(X)
of an orbifold CW complex
X
which is generated by representable maps
M\to X
whose domain is a closed orbifold, equipped with a stable complex structure, modulo bordism over
X
. This is evidently functorial under representable maps, and can be considered as a homology theory on orbispaces (the precise formulation requires introducing a category of pairs, which we omit to simplify the discussion).

A more elaborate notion is that of a
derived orbifold
, namely a triple
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
where
{\mathcal{U}}
is an orbifold and
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a section of a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
. A derived orbifold is called compact if
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
has compact coarse space, and a stable complex structure on
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is a complex structure on the virtual vector bundle
T{\mathcal{U}}-{\mathcal{E}}
, which is exhibited by third vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
and complex structures on
T{\mathcal{U}}\oplus{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
and
T{\mathcal{U}}\oplus{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
. The stably complex derived orbifold group
d\Omega^{U}(X)
of an orbispace
X
is defined to be group generated by compact derived orbifolds with a choice of stable complex structure, whose underlying orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
is equipped with a representable map to
X
, modulo bordisms over
X
. This homology group is naturally graded by the virtual dimension
\dim{\mathcal{U}}-\dim{\mathcal{E}}
. The detailed definition is recalled in Section
8.1
.

Remark 1.17
.

In our implementation the geometric applications discussed above, derived orbifolds are obtained as global quotients by compact Lie groups following
[
AMS21
]
: i.e.
{\mathcal{U}}
is presented as the quotient of a manifold by an action with finite isotropy. A non-trivial result of Pardon
[
Par22
]
implies that all derived orbifolds arise as global quotients. The earlier literature was built instead on the variants of the notion of a Kuranishi spaces
[
FO97
,
Par16
,
AB21
]
, which have their own associated bordism theories, which are not explicitly formulated in the literature. We expect all such theories to be equivalent, and to agree with derived orbifold bordism.

Each closed stably complex orbifold
M
may be considered as a derived orbifold with trivial underlying vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
. The analogue in the presence of boundary implies that there is a tautological map of homology theories from ordinary to derived bordism:

(1.12)

\Omega^{U}\to d\Omega^{U}.

We can now state the orbispace versions of the equivariant results from Section
1.1
. We start with Theorem
1.7
:

Theorem 1.18
.

There is a natural transformation

(1.13)

{\mathcal{Z}}:d\Omega^{U}\to\Omega^{U}

of homology theories on orbispaces respecting the
\Omega^{U}
-module structures, which defines a splitting of the inclusion of orbifold bordism in its derived version.

Remark 1.19
.

This result is apparently stronger than the one we formulated for equivariant bordism theories, as we are asserting linearity with respect to the orbifold bordism group, rather than the bordism group of manifolds as in Theorem
1.7
. This is a consequence of the fact that the natural analogue of the product of orbifolds, in the equivariant setting, is the
external
product, as discussed in Remark
1.8
.

In order to state the analogue of Theorem
1.3
, we need to be able to define orbifold bordism as a homology theory on the category of topological spaces. This can be done by dropping the representability assumption, but, as noted in
[
Par21
]
, it is more convenient to instead work with the functor
R:\mathrm{Top}\to\operatorname{RepOrbTop}
which assigns to each space its product with the universal orbispace, in such a way that the set of homotopy classes of maps
M\to X
from an orbifold to
X
is in bijective correspondence with homotopy classes of representable-maps
M\to R(X)
.

Theorem 1.20
.

There is a natural transformation

(1.14)

\Omega^{U}\circ R\to\Omega^{U}

of homology theories of topological spaces, splitting the inclusion of manifold bordism into its orbifold version, and which respects the module structure over
\Omega^{U}_{*}
.

Combining Theorem
1.18
and Theorem
1.20
, we conclude:

Theorem 1.21
.

There is a natural transformation

(1.15)

{\mathcal{Z}}:d\Omega^{U}\circ R\to\Omega^{U}

of homology theories of topological spaces, whose composite with the tautological map is the identity on manifold bordism. ∎

Remark 1.22
.

This result generalizes the natural transformation of homology theories

(1.16)

\mathcal{FOP}:d\Omega^{U}\to H(-;{\mathbb{Z}})

constructed in
[
BX22b
, Theorem 1.4]
, where
d\Omega^{U}
is written as
\overline{\Omega}^{\mathrm{der},{\mathbb{C}}}
.

Remark 1.23
.

Since there are orbifolds which cannot be represented as global quotients by finite groups, we cannot expect to describe derived orbifold bordism in terms of equivariant bordism theories for finite groups. However, given Pardon’s result
[
Par21
]
relating geometric bordism theory
d\Omega^{U}
to Schwede’s global spectrum
\mathbf{MU}

[
Sch18
]
, from which all equivariant bordism spectra can be recovered, we expect that the above splitting theorem can be interpreted in terms of global homotopy theory, extending the results described in Section
1.1
.

Regarding multiplicative structures, we note that the outcome of
[
AB21
]
is the existence of an
A_{\infty}
ring spectrum representing derived orbifold bordism; forthcoming work will refine this to an
E_{\infty}
ring structure, for which the natural map from complex bordism respects the
E_{\infty}
ring map. A more homotopical model of this spectrum should be closely related to Pardon’s constructions, leading to the following natural question: does this map split as
E_{\infty}
spectra? We expect that the construction of such a map would greatly simplify the putative construction of (Hamiltonian) Floer homotopy type over the complex cobordism, see the discussion in Section
1.5
.

1.4.
Methods of proof

To connect Theorem
1.21
with Theorem
1.14
, we use recent constructions of global Kuranishi charts for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)

[
AMS21
,
HS22
,
AMS23
]
that produce a well-defined class in
d\Omega_{*}^{U}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}\times X^{k})
from the corresponding derived orbifold presentations of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,k}(X,J,A)
. The complex cobordism class which is the subject of Theorem
1.14
is simply obtained from applying the splitting map
{\mathcal{Z}}
to the aforementioned class. Accordingly, we focus on explaining the construction of the splitting.

As alluded to earlier, our construction uses geometric perturbations of the defining section
{\mathcal{S}}
of a compact stably complex derived orbifold
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
, which can be seen as following in the footsteps of the classical symplectic approach to Gromov–Witten theory
[
LT98b
,
FO99
,
Rua99
,
Sie96
,
HWZ17
]
. This is in contrast to the recent constructions of virtual fundamental classes in ordinary and generalized homology theories
[
Par16
,
AB21
,
AMS21
]
, which use Poincaré duality and Thom isomorphisms. The perturbation approach we take is a modification of the Fukaya–Ono–Parker (FOP) perturbation scheme developed by the second author and Xu
[
BX22b
]
, which draws inspiration from
[
FO97
,
Par13
]
. The main difference with the previous work of the second author with Xu is that we do not use the canonical Whitney stratifications, which is not refined enough for us to resolve the singularities of the zero loci of the perturbed sections.

The output of this perturbation will not in general be a manifold, and in fact not even an orbifold. Indeed, the local model is given by a choice of a finite group
\Gamma
, a pair
V
and
W
of finite-dimensional complex representations of
\Gamma
with
V
being faithful, and we have
{\mathcal{U}}=[V/\Gamma]
,
{\mathcal{E}}=[(V\times W)/\Gamma]
. In this situation, the section
{\mathcal{S}}
is determined by a
\Gamma
-equivariant polynomial map
f:V\to W
, and while a generic choice ensures that the zero-locus
f^{-1}(0)
is smooth away from the point in
V
with non-trivial stabilizer, there is no general procedure for guaranteeing that this variety is everywhere smooth. The main insight of this paper is that we can compatibly resolve the resulting singularities. We shall do so in three steps:

We first apply Abramovich–Temkin–Włodarczyk’s
functorial embedded resolution of singularities
algorithm
[
ATW19
]
to build a
compact stably complex orbifold
from a compact stably complex derived orbifold
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
. In the local model, this amounts to resolving the singularities of
f^{-1}(0)
. The functoriality of this algorithm ensures that the resolution is in fact
\Gamma
-equivariant, and the corresponding quotient orbifold is the output of this step. In reality, we need to patch such local constructions together, and figure out a way to deal with the non-algebraic nature of a general
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
. The functoriality of Abramovich–Temkin–Włodarczyk’s method is of essential importance in achieving this.

Remark 1.24
.

Given that Hironaka’s original construction of resolution of singularities was not sufficiently functorial, it seems implausible that we would be able to alternatively use it. On the other hand, we expect to be able to use later proofs of resolution of singularities in the lineage of Abhyankar
[
Abh82
]
, at the cost of additional complexity in our arguments.

The second step is concerned with making all the stabilizers of a given compact stably complex orbifold
abelian
. Our method follows the work of Bergh–Rydh
[
BR19
]
and we apply iterative blow-ups to abelianize the orbifold. Roughly speaking, one defines an integer-valued function on the coarse space of an orbifold to measure ‘how far’ the orbifold is from being abelian, and blows up along the locus over which such a function achieves its maximum.

In the third step, we modify an abelian stably complex orbifold so that the coarse space of the resulting orbifold is a
smooth manifold
. This step heavily relies on Bergh’s functorial destackification result
[
Ber17
]
for algebraic abelian orbifolds, which is rather technical, but the basic idea is simply that the coarse space of the orbifold obtained as the quotient of
\mathbb{C}
by a cyclic group action is naturally identified again with
\mathbb{C}
, and hence is smooth.

Of course, one needs to ensure that each step is compatible with the equivalence relations entering the definition of
d\Omega_{*}^{U}
, and that invariance holds despite the various auxiliary choice.

1.5.
Outlook and further context

Although we state our main result for the homology theory
d\Omega_{*}^{U}
, our construction actually applies to
normally complex orbifolds
. Such a structure on an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
consists of the data of stable complex structures on the normal bundles of all strata, which are compatible with inclusions. If
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is a compact derived orbifold with
{\mathcal{U}}
normally complex and
{\mathcal{E}}
complex, we obtain a cobordism class of smooth manifolds from the algorithms in Section
5.2
and Section
8.2
.

The applicability of our results to normally complex orbifolds opens up much wider applications in symplectic topology. In fact, all the moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic curves appearing in Gromov–Witten theory, Hamiltonian Floer theory, and Lagrangian Floer theory are expected to be presented as the zero loci of the section from certain normally complex derived orbifolds. The essential observation is that all the nontrivial automorphisms of stable maps with at least one marked point are caused by closed irreducible component of the domain, and accordingly, the nontrivial (virtual) representations produced from the equivariant Fredholm indices of the linear deformation operator are complex.

On the Hamiltonian Floer side, the availability of global Kuranishi charts and the package of derived orbifold flow categories together with the results in this paper allows us to envision a Hamiltonian Floer homotopy type as an
MU
-module for general symplectic manifolds. However, due to the failure of multiplicativity under Cartesian products of all the resolution methods we use, our result can not be directly applied to produce such a homotopy type. We expect that a more refined certain variants of our method, adapted to the specific stratifications appearing in Hamiltonian Floer theory, will allow us to obtain such a refinement, and we shall return to this task in the future.

Finally, it is an open problem to define complex bordism/algebraic cobordism valued Gromov–Witten invariants using algebraic geometry building on
[
LT98a
,
BF97
]
. There is a fundamental step in our approach (Lemma
7.6
) which uses a general position argument in the smooth category, so it is unclear if the methods of this paper can be reproduced purely in the algebraic category. Nevertheless, our invariants
(\overline{\mathbf{M}}_{g.k}(X,A),\mathrm{st}\times\mathrm{ev})
are invariants of deformation classes of smooth projective algebraic varieties. Developing calculation tools and exploring structural aspects of these invariants provides another set of open problems.

1.6.
Outline of the paper

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall basic notions about orbifolds, including normal complex structures, and FOP perturbations using the topological stack point of view. In the subsequent sections, we discuss different steps of the resolution of singularities algorithm, ordered in what we perceive to be increasing complexity.

•

Following
[
BR19
]
, we present how to functorially make the stabilizers of a normally complex orbifold abelian by a sequence of ordinary blow-ups in Section
3
.

•

In Section
4
, we adapt the approach in
[
Ber17
]
to our setting to make the coarse space of an abelian normally complex orbifold smooth by a combination of ordinary blow-ups and root stack constructions.

•

Before proceeding to the constructions involving derived orbifolds, we describe, in Section
5.3
, an algorithm using the preceding results to extract a smooth, stably complex manifold, from a stably complex orbifold.

•

After recalling the results on functorial embedded resolution of singularities from
[
ATW19
]
and verifying various implications on the resolutions of the universal zero loci in Section
6
, we show how to combine the FOP perturbation scheme with resolution of singularities to produce a normally complex orbifold from a normally complex derived orbifold in Section
7
.

•

Section
8
assembles the discussion of the preceding two section to associate a stably complex orbifold to a stably complex derived orbifold. The results stated in Section
1.1
follow as corollaries.

•

Finally, in Section
9
, we provide proofs of the applications stated in Section
1.2
.

2.
Normally complex orbifolds

2.1.
Orbifolds and vector bundles

We recall basic notions about orbifolds using the language of topological stacks following the expositions in
[
Par22
,
Par21
]
. We choose not to formulate our construction in terms of Satake and Thurston’s simpler point of view because we cannot avoid introducing ineffective suborbifolds in our subsequent constructions. However, nearly all the orbifolds appearing as ambient spaces are effective, except for setting up different versions of orbifold bordism theory.

Let
\mathbf{Top}
be the category with topological spaces as objects and continuous maps as morphisms. Denote by
\mathbf{Grpd}
the
2
-category of essentially small groupoids. A
stack
is a functor
F:\mathbf{Top}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Grpd}
satisfying descent, namely, for a topological space
U
with an open cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}
, the functor

(2.1)

F(U)\to\mathrm{Eq}\big(F(U_{\alpha})\rightrightarrows F(U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta})\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\\[-2.58334pt]
\textstyle\rightarrow\end{subarray}}F(U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\cap U_{\gamma})\big)

is an equivalence. Stacks form a
2
-category denoted by
\mathrm{Shv}(\mathbf{Top},\mathbf{Grpd})
. The Yoneda embedding
\mathbf{Top}\to\mathrm{Shv}(\mathbf{Top},\mathbf{Grpd})
defined by taking
U\in\mathbf{Top}
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{Top}}(-,U)
is a fully faithful embedding, with (essential) image called
representable
stacks.

A morphism of stacks
{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
is called
representable
if for every topological space
U
, viewed as a stack via Yoneda embedding, and any morphism
U\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, the fiber product
{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U
is representable. Given a property
P
of maps of topological spaces which is preserved under pullback, a representable morphism
{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
is said to satisfy property
P
if for every topological space
U
and any morphism
U\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, the map from the fiber product
{\mathcal{X}}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U\to U
has property
P
. Properties preserved under pullbacks include being injective, surjective, open, a (closed) embedding, separated, proper, admitting local sections, and étale (finite covering space). As an example, we say that
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{Y}}\}
is an
open cover
if each for any topological space
U
and morphism
U\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, each of the map
{\mathcal{X}}_{\alpha}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U\to U
is an open embedding, and the collection of open subsets
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{\alpha}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}U\}
forms an open cover of
U
.

The
coarse space

|{\mathcal{X}}|
of a stack
{\mathcal{X}}
, is the topological space characterized as the initial object in the category of maps from
{\mathcal{X}}
to the Yoneda image of
\mathbf{Top}
, which is equivalently understood as the the image of
{\mathcal{X}}
under the left adjoint of the Yoneda embedding. The coarse space
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is also called the set of
points
on
{\mathcal{X}}
, because it indeed consists of isomorphism classes of morphisms of the form
*\to{\mathcal{X}}
. The
isotropy group
or
stabilizer
of a point
x\in|{\mathcal{X}}|
is the automorphism group of the morphism
*\to{\mathcal{X}}
induced by
x
. A stack is called
compact
if its coarse space
|{\mathcal{X}}|
is a compact topological space.

Definition 2.1
.

(1)

A stack
{\mathcal{X}}
is called
topological
if there exists a topological space
U
and a representable surjective morphism
U\to{\mathcal{X}}
admitting local sections. Under such setting,
U
is called an
atlas
.

(2)

A
(separated) orbispace
is a topological stack
{\mathcal{X}}
admitting an
étale
atlas
U\to{\mathcal{X}}
such that the diagonal morphism
{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}
is proper.

(3)

An orbispace
{\mathcal{X}}
is called a
topological orbifold
if it admits an étale atlas
U\to{\mathcal{X}}
such that
U
is locally homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Here
n
is defined as the dimension of
{\mathcal{X}}
.

(4)

A
(smooth) orbifold
is a topological orbifold
{\mathcal{X}}
equipped with a
smooth structure
: i.e., a choice of étale atlas
U\to{\mathcal{X}}
defining the topological orbifold structure together with a choice of smooth structure on
U
such that the two smooth structures on
U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U
defined by pulling back the smooth structure on
U
via the two projections agree.

(5)

A map of smooth orbifolds
f:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
is called
smooth
if there exist smooth étale atlases
U_{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
and
U_{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
such that
f
can be lifted to a smooth map
U_{\mathcal{X}}\to U_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Topological stacks can be constructed from topological groupoids. Recall that a topological groupoid consists of a pair of topological spaces
M
and
O
, with source and target maps
s,t:M\to O
, composition morphism
m:M\times_{O}M\to M
(here the fiber product is defined using
s
and
t
), as well an an identity morphism
i:O\to M
(the identity map), satisfying the axioms of a groupoid (in particular, the existence of an inverse
\iota:M\to M
). A topological groupoid
M\rightrightarrows O
gives rise to a stack
[M\rightrightarrows O]
as follows: for a topological space
U^{\prime}
, the groupoid associated to
[M\rightrightarrows O](U^{\prime})
has objects given by open covers
\{U^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}
of
U^{\prime}
endowed with maps
U^{\prime}_{\alpha}\to O
and
U^{\prime}_{\alpha}\cap U^{\prime}_{\beta}\to M
satisfying suitable compatibility conditions. The (iso)morphisms of two such
\{U^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}
and
\{U^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\}
are specified by maps
U^{\prime}_{\alpha}\cap U^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
satisfying certain compatibility conditions. It is straightforward to check that
[M\rightrightarrows O]
satisfies descent. The natural map
O\to[M\rightrightarrows O]
is defined by evaluating at
U^{\prime}
with the trivial cover
\{U^{\prime}\}
, and the fiber product
O\times_{[M\rightrightarrows O]}O
is identified with
M
. One can show that
O\to[M\rightrightarrows O]
is representable by applying the local sections of
O\to[M\rightrightarrows O]
to the representable morphism
M\cong O\times_{[M\rightrightarrows O]}O\to O
.

On the other hand, for a topological stack
{\mathcal{X}}
with an atlas
U\to{\mathcal{X}}
, we can look at the topological space
U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U
. Let
s,t:U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U\to U
be the projection map to be first and second component respectively. Let
m:(U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U)\times_{U}(U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U)=U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U\to U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U
be the map forgetting the middle factor, and let
i:U\to U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U
be the diagonal map. This data defines a topological groupoid, with inverse given by the map
\iota:U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U\to U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U
exchanging the two factors, and the topological stack associated to this groupoid
[U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U\rightrightarrows U]
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{X}}
. Indeed, there exists a natural map
[U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U\rightrightarrows U]\to{\mathcal{X}}
by the descent property of
{\mathcal{X}}
which defines an equivalence.

As a classical example, given a topological space
X
acted on continuously by a topological group
G
, the
action groupoid

G\times X\rightrightarrows X
has source and target map given by
(g,x)\mapsto x
and
(g,x)\mapsto g\cdot x
respectively, composition map given by
m:(g_{1},g_{2},x)\mapsto(g_{1}\cdot g_{2},x)
, and identity map
i:x\mapsto(e,x)
. The resulting stack is denoted by
X//G
coming with a morphism
X\to X//G
. The coarse space
|X//G|
is the quotient
X/G
.

Using the equivalence exhibited as above, we see that:

Lemma 2.2
.

A topological groupoid
(M,O,s,t,i,\iota,m)
gives rise an
n
-dimensional smooth orbifold if the following holds:

•

both
M
and
O
are second countable Hausdorff topogical spaces locally homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, equipped with smooth structures, so that the transition functions between charts are smooth;

•

both the source and target map
s,t
are smooth and define local diffeomorphisms (the
étale
property);

•

all the continuous maps
i,\iota,m
are smooth with respect to the above smooth structure;

•

the map
s\times t:M\to O\times O
is proper (the
separatedness
property). ∎

This result makes connection with the exposition in, e.g.,
[
ALR07
, Section 1.4]
, in which this is referred to as a
proper étale Lie groupoid
. Note that in
loc. cit.
, an orbifold is defined to be a topological space
|{\mathcal{X}}|
equipped with a
(Morita) equivalence class
of orbifold structures, i.e., a proper étale Lie groupoid such that the coarse space of the associated topological stack is homeomorphic to
|{\mathcal{X}}|
. In the sequel, we will use the notation
{\mathcal{U}}
to denote smooth orbifolds. We do not distinguish between isomorphic smooth structures on
{\mathcal{U}}
. In the special setting of the action groupoid from a smooth action of a finite group on a smooth manifold, we call the corresponding orbifold a
finite quotient orbifold
.

Proposition 2.3
.

A stack
{\mathcal{U}}
is an orbifold if and only if the coarse space
|{\mathcal{U}}|
is Hausdorff and it admits an open cover by finite quotient orbifolds
\{U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}\}
.

Accordingly, any smooth étale atlas
U\to{\mathcal{U}}
admits a refinement by a covering of finite quotient orbifolds: there exists an open cover
\{U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}\}
such that the map
U_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}
factors through
U_{\alpha}\to U
.

Proof.

This is
[
Par22
, Proposition 3.3, Corollary 3.5]
in the setting of orbifolds.
∎

Using such an open cover, the stabilizer
\Gamma_{x}
of a point
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
is isomorphic to the stabilizer of any preimage of
x
in
U_{\alpha}
of the
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-action if
x\in U_{\alpha}/\Gamma_{\alpha}
. An orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
is called
effective
if it admits an open cover by finite quotient orbifolds
\{U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}\}
such that the
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-action on
U_{\alpha}
is faithful. If
{\mathcal{U}}
is effective, we can further refine any étale atlas such that each
U_{\alpha}
can be identified with a
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-invariant open subset of the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
which is a faithful
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-representation. When this is the case, the open embedding
U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is called an
orbifold chart
.

In our later discussion of orbifold bordism theories, we need to encounter
orbifolds with boundary
, which are locally modeled on
({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})//\Gamma
such that
\Gamma
acts linearly and the action of
\Gamma
on the
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
-factor is trivial. To avoid repeating explanations, we will mostly state definitions and constructions for orbifolds without mentioning the counterparts for orbifolds with boundary unless it is necessary.

Next, we discuss objects living on stacks or orbifolds. A
vector bundle
over a stack
{\mathcal{X}}
is a stack
{\mathcal{E}}
with a representable morphism
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
, which is further endowed with a pair of representable morphisms
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
such that for any topological space
X
with a morphism
X\to{\mathcal{X}}
, there exists an open cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}
of
X
and integers
n_{\alpha}\geq 0
, with the property that for any
\alpha
, the pullback of
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}U_{\alpha}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{\alpha}}\times U_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
and the two morphisms are identified with fiberwise additions and scalar multiplications. A
complex
vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is be defined by replacing
{\mathbb{R}}
by
{\mathbb{C}}
in the above definition. We just need to encounter the cases when all the
n_{\alpha}
are equal. In the case of an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, a
smooth vector bundle
over
{\mathcal{U}}
of rank
k
is defined to be a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
where
{\mathcal{E}}
is a smooth orbifold with smooth projection map, so that for any smooth map from a smooth manifold
X\to{\mathcal{U}}
, there exists an open cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}
of
X
, such that the pullback
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{U}}U_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
is smoothly isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times U_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
. Complex smooth vector bundles can be defined similarly. One can easily formulate operations on vector bundles, including pullback, direct sum, dual, and tensor products, and notions of subbundles, quotient bundles, and bundle morphisms, by “evaluating” the notions along topological spaces. Unless otherwise stated, a vector bundle over an orbifold is always assumed to be smooth in our subsequent discussion.

Continuing our example of finite quotient orbifold, given a smooth manifold
X
acted on smoothly by a finite group
\Gamma
, together with a
\Gamma
-representation
W
, we obtain a smooth vector bundle
(X\times W)//\Gamma\to X//\Gamma
. These are the building blocks of vector bundles. Indeed, given a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
, for any étale atlas
U\to{\mathcal{U}}
, the pullback
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{U}}U
is a locally trivial vector bundle under some open over
\{U_{\alpha}\}
of
U
. By refining the étale atlas
\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}
, using Proposition
2.3
, we can assume that the restriction of the étale chart over
U_{\alpha}
factors through
U_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}
for some finite group
\Gamma_{\alpha}
. By the local triviality of
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{U}}U
, we see that
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{U}}U|_{U_{\alpha}}
is the product bundle
U_{\alpha}\times W_{\alpha}
for some
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-representation
W_{\alpha}
. We will call the corresponding bundle map

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{\alpha}\times W_{\alpha})//\Gamma_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}

a
bundle chart
in line with the more traditional convention.

Lemma 2.4
.

Any orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
admits a tangent bundle
T{\mathcal{U}}
, which is an orbifold with a smooth map
T{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
defining a vector bundle over
{\mathcal{U}}
.

Proof.

Let
U\to{\mathcal{U}}
be a smooth étale atlas, and denote by
[U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U\rightrightarrows U]
the associated groupoid. Using the smooth structure on
U
, we can define its tangent bundle
TU
and the projection map
TU\to U
. The fiber product
U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U
acquires a unique smooth structure by the pullback along either factor, so we can define its tangent bundle
T(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U)
. Then we claim that the following defines a groupoid structure on the pair
T(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U),TU
.

•

s,t:T(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U)\to TU
defined by the differentials of the projection to the two factors respectively;

•

i,\iota
are also defined by taking the differentials of the corresponding maps for the pair
U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U,U
.

•

to define
m:T(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U)\times_{TU}T(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U)\to T(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U)
, note that the domain can be identified with
T((U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U)\times_{U}(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U))
, we can again take the differential.

It is easy to see that they satisfy the requirements of being a smooth orbifold provided that
{\mathcal{U}}
is an orbifold. We denote the associated smooth orbifold by
T{\mathcal{U}}
.

The map of stacks
T{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is constructed by observing that the projection maps
T(U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U)\to U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U
and
TU\to U
is compatible with the groupoid structures. The fiberwise additions and scalar multiplications on the smooth manifolds in the above construction descend to representable maps of stacks. Finally, the smoothness of pullback can be checked étale locally.
∎

A smooth map of orbifolds
f:{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}
induces a smooth bundle map
df:T{\mathcal{U}}\to T{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
covering
f
. This can be seen again by looking at the associated groupoids.

Definition 2.5
.

(1)

A smooth map of orbifolds is called an
immersion
if its lift to some (equivalently any) étale atlas is an immersion.

(2)

Given an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, a
suborbifold
is an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
together with a smooth immersion
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}
which defines an isomorphism onto a closed substack of
{\mathcal{U}}
.

(3)

For a suborbifold
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
in an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, its
normal bundle

N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
is defined to be the quotient vector bundle
T{\mathcal{U}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}/T{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}
.

We complete this section by introducing the main objects that we study:

Definition 2.6
.

A
derived orbifold (with boundary)
is a triple
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
in which
{\mathcal{U}}
is a orbifold (with boundary),
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is a vector bundle, and
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a smooth section. Such a derived orbifold is called
compact
if
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
is compact. The
dimension
of such
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is
\dim{\mathcal{U}}-\dim{\mathcal{E}}
.

Given a derived orbifold (with boundary)
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
, we have the following two operations.

•

Restriction
: if
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
is an open suborbifold containing
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
, we see that
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}},{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}})
is also a derived orbifold (with boundary).

•

Stabilization
: if
\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}:{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is a vector bundle, denote by
\tau_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}:{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\to\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
the tautological section. Then
({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},\pi^{*}{\mathcal{S}}\oplus\tau_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}})
is also a derived orbifold (with boundary).

Note that the zero locus is not changed by either restriction or stabilization. Therefore, restrictions and stabilizations of compact derived orbifold remain compact. For notational convenience, we will use
{\mathcal{D}}=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
to denote a derived orbifold (with boundary).

2.2.
Differential geometry on orbifolds: metrics and normal complex structures

We shall be using, within the setting of orbifolds, some basic notions of differential geometry. To begin, we introduce the following:

Definition 2.7
.

(1)

A
metric
on a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is a map
g:{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying
g(w_{1},w_{2})=g(w_{2},w_{1})
,
g(w_{1},r\cdot w_{2})=r\cdot g(w_{1},w_{2})
for
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
g(w,w)\geq 0
with equality if and only if
w=0
.

(2)

A
hermitian metric
on a complex vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is a map
h:{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{E}}
satisfying
h(w_{1},w_{2})=\overline{h(w_{2},w_{1})}
,
h(w_{1},c\cdot w_{2})=c\cdot h(w_{1},w_{2})
for
c\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
h(w,w)\geq 0
with equality if and only if
w=0
.

As usual, a metric on
T{\mathcal{U}}
is called a
Riemannian metric
. The exponential map of any smooth étale atlas descends to a unique smooth map
\exp:T{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
, also referred to as the
exponential map
. Other differential-geometric notions, including connections, parallel transport, geodesics, etc, can also be defined for orbifolds in the natural way.

Next, we introduce the following structure which is particularly interesting from the point of view of symplectic topology, as it arises in the study of various (thickened) moduli spaces of
J
-holomorphic curves. From a technical point of view, this is the most natural class of orbifolds to which one can apply algebro-geometric methods to simplify the singularities of the coarse spaces, as we will see in later sections.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold, and suppose that
\Gamma
is a finite group acting smoothly on
M
. For any subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, denote by
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
the fixed point locus of the restricted
\Gamma^{\prime}
-action. A
normal complex structure
on the
\Gamma
-manifold
M
is a collection of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant complex structures on the normal bundle

(2.3)

I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}:NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

for all
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
satisfying the following compatibility condition: for any pair of subgroups
\Gamma_{2}<\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma
the embedding of vector bundles

(2.4)

NM^{\Gamma_{2}}|_{M^{\Gamma_{1}}}\to NM^{\Gamma_{1}}

along
M^{\Gamma_{1}}
respects the complex structures, and the canonical splitting

(2.5)

NM^{\Gamma_{1}}\cong NM^{\Gamma_{2}}|_{M^{\Gamma_{1}}}\oplus(NM^{\Gamma_{2}}|_{M^{\Gamma_{1}}})^{\perp}

as the fiberwise direct sum of
\Gamma_{2}
-representations is preserved by
I_{\Gamma_{1}}
.

Using the splitting (
2.5
), the above definition asks that the two resulting complex structures from
I_{\Gamma_{1}}
and
I_{\Gamma_{2}}
on
NM^{\Gamma_{2}}|_{M^{\Gamma_{1}}}
agree.

Given a group homomorphism
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
, and an equivariant smooth open embedding
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
from a
\Gamma_{1}
-manifold
M_{1}
to a
\Gamma_{2}
-manifold
M_{2}
, we may pull back a normal complex strucure on
M_{2}
to a normal complex structure on
M_{1}
, assuming that
f
defines an injection on the stabilizers:

Definition 2.9
.

Given a smooth orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, a
normal complex structure
consists of:

(1)

an étale atlas of the form
U=\coprod U_{\alpha}
for which each
U_{\alpha}
is a smooth manifold acted on smoothly by a finite group
\Gamma_{\alpha}
, such that
\{U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\}
is a cover of
{\mathcal{U}}
by open substacks;

(2)

a normal complex structure on each
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-manifold
U_{\alpha}
such that the two normal complex structures on
U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U
obtained from the pullback of two projections maps are isomorphic.

Remark 2.10
.

Normal complex structures and their appearance in symplectic topology were first discovered by Fukaya–Ono in
[
FO97
]
. The first systematic investigation appeared in
[
BX22b
]
, and the definition from there is stated for effective orbifolds using the language of orbifold atlas, which we recall for the sake of concreteness. For an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, viewed as a Hausdorff space
|{\mathcal{U}}|
equiped with an equivalence class of orbifold atlases, a normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
is a collection of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant complex structures on the normal bundle of the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-fixed point locus
U^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
respecting the fiberwise
\Gamma^{\prime}
-action on each orbifold chart
\{(U,\Gamma,\psi)\}
with
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma

(2.6)

I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}:NU^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to NU^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

subject to the following requirements:

(1)

for each orbifold chart
(U,\Gamma)
and
y\in U
with stabilizer
\Gamma_{y}
, suppose we have a pair of subgroups
\Gamma_{2}<\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma_{y}
, then for the
I_{\Gamma_{1}}
-compatible decomposition

(2.7)

N_{y}U^{\Gamma_{1}}=N_{y}U^{\Gamma_{2}}\oplus(TU^{\Gamma_{2}}\cap N_{y}U^{\Gamma_{1}})

of
\Gamma_{1}
-representations, the restriction of
I_{\Gamma_{1}}
along the component
N_{y}U^{\Gamma_{2}}
coincides with the complex structure
I_{\Gamma_{2}}
;

(2)

if we have a chart embedding
\psi_{12}:(U_{1},\Gamma_{1})\to(U_{2},\Gamma_{2})
, and for
y\in U_{1}
, we identify the representatives of the stabilizer of
\psi_{1}(y)=\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{12}(y)
given by
\Gamma_{y}
and
\Gamma_{\psi(y)}
under the group injection
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
, then the differential
d\psi_{12}:NU_{1}^{\Gamma_{y}}\to NU_{2}^{\Gamma_{\psi(y)}}
intertwines with the complex structures
I_{\Gamma_{y}}
and
I_{\Gamma_{\psi(y)}}
.

It is straightforward to see that this is equivalent to Definition
2.9
, as the compatibility condition (2) is encoded in the requirement (2) of Definition
2.9
.

A normally complex orbifold is defined to be an orbifold equipped with an isomorphism class of normal complex structures. Note that
any
manifold endowed with the trivial orbifold structure is automatically a normally complex orbifold. For another class of examples, recall that an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
is called
almost complex
if its tangent bundle
T{\mathcal{U}}
is equipped with an isomorphism class of complex structures. Then any almost complex orbifold is also normally complex. Smooth Deligne–Mumford stacks over
{\mathbb{C}}
produce examples of almost complex orbifolds.

A choice of normal complex structure provides a finer refinement of an orbifold than the standard stratification associated to the isotropy group, by including the choice of representation:

Definition 2.11
.

The set of
isotropy types
is the set of equivalence classes of pairs

(2.8)

(\Gamma,V),

where
\Gamma
is a finite group and
V
is a finite-dimensional complex
\Gamma
-representation which does not contain any trivial summand. Here
(\Gamma,V)
is declared to be equivalent to
(\Gamma^{\prime},V^{\prime})
if there is an isomorphism of groups
\Gamma\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma^{\prime}
under which
V
and
V^{\prime}
are isomorphic representations.

Remark 2.12
.

The standard terminology is to use isotropy to refer only to the choice of group, but the more refine decomposition will be essential for out construction, and is consistent with the formulation in
[
BX22b
]
.

The set of isotropy types admits a natural partial order as follows. We say that
(\Gamma^{\prime},V^{\prime})\leq(\Gamma,V)
if there exists an injective homomorphism
\Gamma^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\Gamma
such that
V^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the quotient of
V
by its
\Gamma^{\prime}
-fixed subspace.

Definition 2.13
.

Given a normally complex orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, the
normal invariant
at
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
is the isotropy type of the pair

(2.9)

(\Gamma_{y},N_{y}U^{\Gamma_{y}})

where
U//\Gamma\to{\mathcal{U}}
is smoothly embedded as an open substack and
x
is the image of
y
under
U/\Gamma\to|{\mathcal{U}}|
.

It is easy to see that different choices of étale charts covering
x
and different choices of inverse images of
x
, define equivalent isotropy types. Therefore, the normal invariant of
x\in{\mathcal{U}}
is well-defined.

In order to formulate the notion of normally complex derived orbifold, we need the notion of a normally complex vector bundle. We again start with the equivariant setting.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
M
be a smooth
\Gamma
-manifold and suppose
E\to M
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant vector bundle. Given
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, introduce the notation

(2.10)

E|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}=\mathring{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\oplus\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

which decomposes each fiber of
E|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
as a direct sum of trivial and nontrivial
\Gamma^{\prime}
-representations. Then a
normal complex structure
on
E
is a collection of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant complex structures

(2.11)

J_{\Gamma^{\prime}}:\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

for all
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
such that for any pair of subgroups
\Gamma_{2}<\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma
, the embedding of vector bundles

(2.12)

\check{E}^{\Gamma_{1}}|_{M^{\Gamma}_{2}}\to\check{E}^{\Gamma_{2}},

along
M^{\Gamma_{2}}
is a map of complex vector bundles, and the canonical decomposition
\check{E}^{\Gamma_{2}}=\check{E}^{\Gamma_{1}}|_{M^{\Gamma_{2}}}\oplus(\mathring{E}^{\Gamma_{1}}\cap\check{E}^{\Gamma_{2}})
is preserved by the complex structure
J_{\Gamma_{2}}
.

As before, given a group homomorphism
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
, the pullback of a normally complex vector bundle on under an equivariant smooth open embedding
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
from a
\Gamma_{1}
-manifold
M_{1}
to a
\Gamma_{2}
-manifold
M_{2}
, has a natural normally complex structure whenever
f
induces an injection on the stabilizer groups.

Remark 2.15
.

The fixed point locus
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
may have different connected components with different dimensions, and the objects
\mathring{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
and
\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
only define vector bundles over these connected components. To simplify the notations, we will stick to these slightly imprecise notations which should not cause confusions.

Definition 2.16
.

A
normal complex structure
on a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
consists of:

(1)

an étale atlas of
{\mathcal{E}}
of the form
E=\coprod_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}
covering an étale atlas of
{\mathcal{U}}
of the form
\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}
as a vector bundle such that:

•

U_{\alpha}
is a smooth
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-manifold,
E_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
is a
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-equivariant vector bundle,
E_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is an open embedding which is further a bundle map covering an open embedding
U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}
;

•

E_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
is equipped with a normal complex structure;

(2)

for the vector bundle
E\times_{\mathcal{E}}E\to U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U
, the two normal complex structures induced from the pullback of two projection maps
U\times_{\mathcal{U}}U\to U
agree.

Remark 2.17
.

Continuing Remark
2.10
, we recall the arguably more concrete formulation of normal complex structures on vector bundles in terms of bundle charts as appeared in
[
BX22b
]
.
For a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
, a normal complex structure is a collection of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant complex structures

(2.13)

J_{\Gamma^{\prime}}:\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

for each bundle chart
(U,\Gamma,E)
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
subject to the following conditions:

(1)

for each bundle chart
(U,\Gamma,E)
and
y\in U
with stabilizer
\Gamma_{y}
, suppose we have a pair of subgroups
\Gamma_{2}<\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma_{y}
, then for the
J_{\Gamma_{1}}
-compatible decomposition

(2.14)

\check{E}_{y}^{\Gamma_{1}}=\check{E}_{y}^{\Gamma_{2}}\oplus(\mathring{E}_{y}^{\Gamma_{2}}\cap\check{E}_{y}^{\Gamma_{1}})

of
\Gamma_{1}
-representations, the restriction of
J_{\Gamma_{1}}
along the component
\check{E}_{y}^{\Gamma_{2}}
coincides with the complex structure
J_{\Gamma_{2}}
;

(2)

if we have a bundle chart embedding
\hat{\psi}_{12}:(U_{1},\Gamma_{1},E_{1})\to(U_{2},\Gamma_{2},E_{2})
, and for
y\in U_{1}
, we identify the representatives of the stabilizer of
\psi_{1}(y)=\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{12}(y)
given by
\Gamma_{y}
and
\Gamma_{\psi(y)}
under the group injection
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
, then the bundle map
\hat{\psi}_{12}:\check{E}_{1}^{\Gamma_{y}}\to\check{E}_{2}^{\Gamma_{\psi(y)}}
intertwines with the complex structures
J_{\Gamma_{y}}
and
J_{\Gamma_{\psi(y)}}
.

The constructions of this paper are formulated for the following class of derived orbifolds:

Definition 2.18
.

A
normal complex structure
on a derived orbifold
{\mathcal{D}}=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
(with boundary) consists of normal complex structures on
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{E}
.

Of course, restrictions of derived orbifolds preserves normal complex structure, so does the stabilization by complex vector bundles.

2.3.
Fukaya–Ono–Parker sections

In this subsection, we recall the definition of
normally complex polynomial perturbations/sections
introduced by Fukaya–Ono
[
FO97
]
and Parker
[
Par13
]
. Following
[
BX22b
]
, we call such sections the Fukaya–Ono–Parker (FOP) sections.

We need to discuss a distinguished class of metric and connection structures on orbifolds and vector bundles to get prepared for our discussion of the above distinguished class of sections.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold, and suppose that
\Gamma
is a finite group acting smoothly on
M
. For any subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, let
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
be the fixed point locus of the restricted
\Gamma^{\prime}
-action. Choosing a
\Gamma
-equivariant Riemannian metric
g
on
M
, then the splitting

(2.15)

TM|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\cong TM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\oplus NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}},

equipps
\pi_{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}:NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
with a bundle metric and a connection from the Levi-Civita connection. Using the splitting
T(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})=(\pi_{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}})^{*}TM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\oplus(\pi_{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}})^{*}NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
induced by the connection, the bundle metric on
NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
and the induced Riemannian metric on
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
together equip
NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
with a Riemannian metric
g^{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
. On the other hand, the normal exponential map

(2.16)

\exp:NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to M

defines a diffeomorphism onto its image when restricted along a sufficiently small open neighborhood of the
0
-section
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\subset M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Definition 2.19
.

A
\Gamma
-equivariant Riemannian metric
g
on
M
is called (normally)
straightened
if for any
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, the push-forward of the metric
g^{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
on
NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
under the normal exponential map agrees with the restriction of
g
to an open neighborhood of
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
over which
\exp
defines a diffeomorphism.

A straightened metric has the following feature: given a group
\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma
, consider the decomposition of vector bundles over
M^{\Gamma_{1}}

(2.17)

NM^{\Gamma_{1}}=(\mathring{NM^{\Gamma_{1}}})^{\Gamma_{2}}\oplus(\check{NM^{\Gamma_{1}}})^{\Gamma_{2}}

associated to the restriction of the fiberwise
\Gamma_{1}
action to a subgroup
\Gamma_{2}<\Gamma_{1}
, where the first component is the
\Gamma_{2}
-trivial component. The normal exponential map defines

(2.18)

\exp:(\mathring{NM^{\Gamma_{1}}})^{\Gamma_{2}}\to M^{\Gamma_{2}}.

Given
y\in M^{\Gamma_{1}}
and a vector in
N_{y}M^{\Gamma_{1}}
written as
(\mathring{v}_{\Gamma_{2}},\check{v}_{\Gamma_{2}})
under the decomposition of Equation (
2.17
), one can check that

(2.19)

\exp_{x}(\mathring{v}_{\Gamma_{2}},\check{v}_{\Gamma_{2}})=\exp_{\exp_{x}(\mathring{v}_{\Gamma_{2}})}(\check{v}_{\Gamma_{2}}).

In other words, if the metric
g
is straightened, the induced metric along the normal directions behaves like an equivariant flat metric.

Definition 2.20
.

Given a smooth orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, a
straightened Riemannian metric
on
{\mathcal{U}}
is a Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{U}}
such that

(1)

there exists an étale atlas of the form
U=\coprod U_{\alpha}
for which each
U_{\alpha}
is a smooth manifold acted on smoothly by a finite group
\Gamma_{\alpha}
, such that
\{U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\}
is a cover of
{\mathcal{U}}
by open substacks;

(2)

the pullback of
g
to each
U_{\alpha}
defines a straightened
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-equivariant Riemannian metric.

The existence of straightened Riemannian metrics and a relative form for the inductive construction is established by
[
BX22b
, Lemma 3.15]
. The reader can refer to the cited reference for a full proof.

We need a similar structure for vector bundles. Let
M
be a smooth manifold with a
\Gamma
-action, and let
E\to M
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant vector bundle equipped with a
\Gamma
-equivariant connection
\nabla
. Suppose that
M
is endowed with a
\Gamma
-equivariant straightened Riemannian metric
g
. For a subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, we have the projection
\pi_{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}:NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. Over a disc bundle
D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})\subset NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, the parallel transport along normal geodesics induces a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant bundle isomorphism

(2.20)

\rho^{\Gamma^{\prime}}:(\pi_{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}})^{*}(E|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}})\cong E|_{D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})}.

The splitting of
E|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
as a direct sum
E|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}=\mathring{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\oplus\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
from Equation(
2.10
), extends to a splitting

(2.21)

E|_{D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})}=\mathring{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\oplus\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}.

using the isomorphism of Equation (
2.20
).

Definition 2.21
.

For a
\Gamma
-manifold
M
endowed with a
\Gamma
-equivariant straightened Riemannian metric and a
\Gamma
-equivariant vector bundle
E\to M
endowed with a
\Gamma
-equivariant connection
\nabla
, the connection is said to be
straightened
if for any
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, after identifying a disc bundle
D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})
with an open subset of
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
under the exponential map, we have

(2.22)

(\rho^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{*}(\pi_{NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}})^{*}\nabla|_{E|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}}=\nabla|_{E|_{D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})}}.

Again, straightened connections can be pulled back by equivariant smooth open maps which define an injection on the stabilizers.

Definition 2.22
.

A
straightening
on a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
consists of a straightend Riemannian metric
g
on
{\mathcal{U}}
and a connection
\nabla
on
{\mathcal{E}}
such that:

(1)

we have an étale atlas of
{\mathcal{E}}
of the form
E=\coprod_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}
covering an étale atlas of
{\mathcal{U}}
of the form
\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}
as a vector bundle such that:

•

U_{\alpha}
is a smooth
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-manifold,
E_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
is a
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-equivariant vector bundle,
E_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is an open embedding which is further a bundle map covering an open embedding
U_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}
;

•

the straightened Riemannian metric is defined with respect to the atlas
\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}
;

(2)

the pullback of
\nabla
to each
E_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
defines a straightened
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-equivariant connection under the induced straightened
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-equivariant metric on
U_{\alpha}
.

Straightenings of
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
are used to obtain decompositions as in Equation (
2.21
). Their existence and a relative form of the construction is the content of
[
BX22b
, Lemma 3.20]
.

If
\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}:{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is another vector bundle, consider the
stabilization

\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
. If
\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}:{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}
comes with a straightening,
[
BX22b
]
specifies a class of bundle metrics on
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
which is also called straightened. Such a structure can be used to equip the stabilization
\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
with a straightening if furthermore,
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
has a straightening, see
[
BX22b
, Section 3.4]
. This fact will be used in the study of derived orbifold bordisms.

With these preparations, we can proceed to define FOP sections following
[
BX22b
, Section 3.6]
. As usual, we discuss the corresponding notion in the equivariant setting first.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite group and let
(V,W)
be a pair of finite-dimensional complex
\Gamma
-representations. Given a positive integer
d
, denote by

(2.23)

\mathrm{Poly}_{d}^{\Gamma}(V,W)

the space of
\Gamma
-equivariant polynomial maps from
V
to
W
of degree at most
d
. It admits an action of
GL(V)^{\Gamma}\times GL(W)^{\Gamma}
by automorphisms, where
GL(V)^{\Gamma}
resp.
GL(W)^{\Gamma}
denotes elements in
GL(V)
resp.
GL(W)
invariant under the
\Gamma
-conjugation action.

Suppose that
M
is a smooth
\Gamma
-manifold with a normal complex structure in the sense of Definition
2.8
and a straightened Riemannian metric in the sense of Definition
2.19
. Furthermore, suppose that we are given a
\Gamma
-equivariant vector bundle
E\to M
equipped with a normal complex structure (cf. Definition
2.14
) and a straightened connection (cf. Definition
2.21
). Given
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
and a connected component of the fixed point locus
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, any fiber of
NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
defines the same complex
\Gamma^{\prime}
-representation and we write it as
V^{\prime}
. Similarly, using the decomposition of Equation (
2.10
), we denote the fibers of
\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
by
W^{\prime}
as a complex
\Gamma
-representation. Using the structure map of the complex vector bundles
NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
and
\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, we can define a vector bundle

(2.24)

\mathrm{Poly}_{d}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}},\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})

over
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
whose fibers are given by
\mathrm{Poly}_{d}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})
.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
E\to M
be as above. Let
S:M\to E
be a smooth
\Gamma
-equivariant section, which is decomposed as

(2.25)

S=(\mathring{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}},\check{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}):D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})\to\mathring{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\oplus\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

over the disc bundle
D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})
. The section
S
is said to have a
normally complex lift
of degree at most
d
near
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
if there exists a smooth bundle map from a disc bundle

(2.26)

\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}:D_{r}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}})\to\mathrm{Poly}_{d}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}},\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})

such that
\check{S}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\mathrm{ev}\circ\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, where
\mathrm{ev}
is the bundle map

(2.27)

NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\mathrm{Poly}_{d}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}(NM^{\Gamma^{\prime}},\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})\to\check{E}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

defined by the fiberwise evaluation map. If the section
S
admits such a lift near
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
for all
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, we say
S
has a normally complex lift of degree at most
d
.

In this definition, the straightenings are used to make various identifications, so the notion of normally complex lift depends on such structures. For a subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}<\Gamma^{\prime}
, it can be verified that a normally complex lift of degree at most
d
near
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
defines a normally complex lift of degree at most
d
near
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\cap M^{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}
, so lifts near different fixed point loci are compatible.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be a vector bundle. Suppose that both
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
have a normal complex structure and that
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is endowed with a straightening in the sense of Definition
2.22
. Assume that the normal complex structures and the straightening are both specified using étale atlases
E=\coprod_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}
and
U=\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}
as in Definition
2.22
. A section
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is said to have a
normally complex lift
of degree at most
d
if, for each chart
\alpha
, its pullback to the
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-equivariant vector bundle
E_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
admits a normally complex lift of degree at most
d
. If this is the case, we call
{\mathcal{S}}
an
FOP section
.

Remark 2.25
.

Note that any two étale atlases of
{\mathcal{U}}
depicted in Proposition
2.3
can be refined to a common étale atlas of the same form, and a similar statement holds for étale atlases of
{\mathcal{U}}
formed by bundle charts. From this, one notices that the notions of normal complex structures and straightenings do not really depend on the choices of étale atlas in the definition. A particular choice of étale atlas just makes the definition transparent. Similarly, the existence of normally complex lift of
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
does not depend on the choice of étale atlases, though it is important to notice that it does depend on both the normal complex structures and the straightenings prescribed in the definition.

We finally state some properties of FOP sections, whose proof can be found in
[
BX22b
, Section 3.6]
.

Proposition 2.26
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be a vector bundle such that both
