{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
are normally complex, and
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is endowed with a straightening.

(1)

The space of FOP sections is a module over
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{U}})
.

(2)

Suppose that we have an arbitrary continuous section
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
such that
|{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)|
is compact. Let
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
be an open suborbifold containing
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
with
|{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}|
being precompact. Then there exists
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
so that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a smooth section
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
which is an FOP section of degree at most
d
near
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
such that

(2.28)

\|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}-{\mathcal{S}}\|_{C^{0}({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})}<\epsilon.

∎

The importance of (1) is that we can use cut-off functions to define FOP sections in a local-to-global fashion. The item (2) allows us to approximate arbitrary sections by FOP sections, which are crucial for establishing equivariant transversality.

3.
Abelianizing normally complex orbifolds

In this section, we follow Bergh–Rydh
[
BR19
]
to perform blow-ups of normally complex orbifolds to obtain normally complex orbifolds so that the representation associated to each point is a direct sum of
1
-dimensional representations (in the terminology of
[
BR19
]
, this is formulated as abelianess of
geometric stabilizers
). The situation in
[
BR19
]
is more complicated than ours, because the arguments in
loc. cit.
are designed to handle families of possibly non-Deligne–Mumford stacks over a field of arbitrary characteristic.

The strategy of Bergh–Rydh is quite straightforward, and relies on measuring the ‘non-abelianness’ of a normally complex orbifold as follows: the stabilizer group
\Gamma_{x}
of a point
x
in an orbifold
\mathcal{U}
acts on the tangent space
T_{x}{\mathcal{U}}
. Associate to this point the dimension of the direct sum of all irreducible complex representations of complex dimension at least
2
. Note that if the invariant takes value
0
everywhere on
{\mathcal{U}}
, then if
{\mathcal{U}}
is effective, and all the stabilizers inject into a product of
U(1)
, which means that all the stabilizers are abelian. More generally, assuming that
{\mathcal{U}}
is connected with generic stabilizer
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
, every stabilizer is an extension of an abelian group by
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
.

3.1.
Blow-ups

Due to the absence of holomorphic structures, constructing blow-up in the almost complex category such that the blow-down map is pseudo-holomorphic is a delicate question, which indeed cannot be achieved in general. Our construction of blow-up simply specifies a contractible family of normally complex orbifolds, each of which admits a smooth map onto the normally complex orbifolds we started with. In particular, the cobordism class of our blow-up construction is canonically defined and this suffices for our purpose.

Given an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
and a complex vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
, denote by
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\to{\mathcal{U}}
the projectivization of
{\mathcal{E}}
. Then the blow-up of the total space
{\mathcal{E}}
along the zero-section
{\mathcal{U}}
can be described as the orbifold

(3.1)

\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{E}}:=\{(v,l)\in{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\times_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{E}}|l\text{ is contained in the complex line defined by }v\},

where the description can easily be understood by pulling back to étale charts. The projectivization
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})
embeds into
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{E}}
as the locus where
l=0
, known as the exceptional divisor. Moreover, there is a natural projection/blow-down map
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
defined by
(v,l)\mapsto l
, which induces an identification
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{E}}\setminus{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathcal{E}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}
. In general, if we would like to blow up an orbifold along a suborbifold, we need to require the normal bundle of the given suborbifold to have a complex structure. Additionally, in order to stay in the realm of smooth normally complex orbifolds, we need certain conditions to ensure that the blow-up admits a normal complex structure. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth
\Gamma
-manifold and suppose that
g
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant Riemannian metric on
M
. A normal complex structure (cf. Definition
2.8
) is called
flattened
with respect to
g
if there exists
\delta>0
such that for any subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, the (linearization of) normal exponential map over the
\delta
-disc bundle of the normal bundle of
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}

(3.2)

\exp:D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to M

intertwines with the normal complex structures under the group homomorphism
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
.

Similarly, given a smooth orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
and a Riemannian metric
g
on
{\mathcal{U}}
, a normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
(cf. Definition
2.9
) is called
flattened
with respect to
g
if its pullbacks to the defining étale charts are straightened with respect to the pullback equivariant Riemannian metric.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that
M
is a smooth
\Gamma
-manifold equipped with a normal complex structure. Let
g
be a
\Gamma
-invariant Riemannian metric on
M
such that for any
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
, the normal exponential map along
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
has positive injectivity radius. Then the given normal complex structure can be deformed to a flattened one with respect to
g
. Moreover, the space of such flattened Riemannian metrics are contractible.

Proof.

The construction is based on induction. We define a partial order on the set of subgroups of
\Gamma^{\prime}
as follows: for
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\leq\Gamma
, we declare
\Gamma_{1}\preceq\Gamma_{2}
if and only if
M^{\Gamma_{1}}\subset M^{\Gamma_{2}}
.

Staring from any
\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma
such that
\Gamma_{1}
is minimal under the relation
\preceq
, we see that
M^{\Gamma_{1}}
is a closed submanifold and the normal bundle
NM^{\Gamma_{1}}
has a
\Gamma_{1}
-equivariant complex structure. Choose
\delta>0
so that
\exp:D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}}\to M
defines a
\Gamma_{1}
-equivariant diffeomorphism onto its image. To construct a straightened normal complex structure near
M^{\Gamma_{1}}
, we need to make sure that for any
\Gamma_{1}\preceq\Gamma_{2}
, the linearization of the exponential map along the normal directions of the
\Gamma_{2}
fixed point loci

(3.3)

\exp:N_{D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}}}(D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}})^{\Gamma_{2}}\to NM^{\Gamma_{2}}

intertwines with the fiberwise
\Gamma_{2}
-equivariant complex structures. For such a pair
\Gamma_{1}\preceq\Gamma_{2}
, let us denote the complex structure on
N_{D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}}}(D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}})^{\Gamma_{2}}
by
J_{0}
and the pullback of the complex structure on
NM^{\Gamma_{2}}
under
\exp
by
J
. Here we view both
J_{0}
and
J
as
\Gamma_{2}
-equivariant endomorphisms of the vector bundle
N_{D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}}}(D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}})^{\Gamma_{2}}
whose square is
-\mathrm{id}
. Then we claim that there exists a
\Gamma_{2}
-equivariant bundle isomorphism

(3.4)

I_{J_{0},J}:(N_{D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}}}(D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}})^{\Gamma_{2}},J_{0})\to(N_{D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}}}(D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}})^{\Gamma_{2}},J)

of the form
I_{J_{0},J}=\mathrm{id}+\frac{1}{2}J_{0}A_{J_{0},J}
which pulls back
J
to
J_{0}
, where
A_{J_{0},J}
is a
\Gamma_{2}
-equivariant endomorphism which anti-commutes with
J_{0}
. Indeed, explicitly, we can write down the formula

(3.5)

A_{J_{0},J}=-2J_{0}(\mathrm{id}+J_{0}J)(\mathrm{id}-J_{0}J)^{-1}.

Now if we choose a smooth
\Gamma_{2}
-invariant function

(3.6)

\varphi:N_{M^{\Gamma_{2}}}M^{\Gamma_{1}}\to{\mathbb{R}}

which is equal to
0
near the
0
-section and is equal to
1
outside a small disc bundle over the
0
-section, we can define a
\Gamma_{2}
-equivariant complex structure on
N_{D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}}}(D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}})^{\Gamma_{2}}
by the formula

(3.7)

(\mathrm{id}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi J_{0}A_{J_{0},J})\circ J_{0}\circ(\mathrm{id}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi J_{0}A_{J_{0},J})^{-1}.

If we push forward such a complex structure to
NM^{\Gamma_{2}}
and extend it by the original normal complex structure, based on the construction, we see that it is straightened with respect to
g
along
M^{\Gamma_{2}}\cap\exp(D_{\delta}M^{\Gamma_{1}})
. If we range over all
\Gamma_{1}\preceq\Gamma_{2}
, and perform such a cut-off construction using induction based on the induced partial orders on the set of all such
\Gamma_{2}
, the compatibility of different cut-off constructions makes sure that the normal complex structure can be straightened near
M^{\Gamma_{1}}
.

Then we can prove the existence by induction using the partial order
\preceq
. The details are similar to the proof of
[
BX22b
, Lemma 3.15]
, to which we refer the reader. By construction, such a newly constructed normal complex structure can be deformed to the original one by varying the cut-off functions. Note that the only choice we make in the construction is a sequence of cut-off functions. For different choices, we can convexly interpolate between the resulting straightened normal complex structures upon restricting to smaller open subsets containing the fixed point loci. The contractibility of the space of our straightened normal complex structures follows directly.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

If
{\mathcal{U}}
is a compact orbifold with a normal complex structure, then given any Riemannian metric
g
on
{\mathcal{U}}
, there exists a contractible family of flattened normal complex structures on
{\mathcal{U}}
with respect to
g
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
3.2
. However, the induction is based on the following partial order on the set of isotropy types (cf. Definition
2.11
): we declare
(\Gamma_{1},V_{1})\preceq(\Gamma_{2},V_{2})
if and only if
\Gamma_{2}\leq\Gamma_{1}
and
V_{2}
is the complement of
\Gamma_{2}
-invariant subspace of
V_{1}
. Such a partial order induces a partial order on the set of normal invariants of
{\mathcal{U}}
(cf. Definition
2.13
), which leads to a filtration of
{\mathcal{U}}
by closed subspaces. Lemma
3.2
describes the construction over étale charts, which can be globalized for orbifolds.
∎

Definition 3.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be an orbifold with a normal complex structure. Suppose that
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
is a suborbifold such that
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
admits a complex structure. The complex structure on
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
is
compatible
with the normal complex structure if for any
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}|
and an open embedding
U//\Gamma\to{\mathcal{U}}
with
y\mapsto x
under
U/\Gamma\to|{\mathcal{U}}|
, the normal space of the preimage of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
in
U
at
y
with the induced complex structure from
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
is a complex subspace of
N_{y}U^{\Gamma_{y}}
, which is endowed with the complex structure from the normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
.

In practice, we will construct complex structures on
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
using the normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
using concrete geometric information. Under the situation of Definition
3.4
, we will also say
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
is a suborbifold
with complex normal bundle
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be a compact orbifold with a normal complex structure, which is flattened with respect to a Riemannian metric
g
on
{\mathcal{U}}
. Suppose that
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
is a suborbifold whose normal bundle
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
admits a complex structure compatible with the normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
. The
blow-up
of
{\mathcal{U}}
along
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
, written as
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}
, is defined to be the pushout of the diagram

(3.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{\delta}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})|_{D_{\delta}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
inclusion

which comes with a map
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
called the
blow-down
map. Here
\delta>0
is chosen so that the normal exponential map over the disc bundle
D_{\delta}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
defines an open embedding of normal complex orbifolds, and
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})|_{D_{\delta}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}
denotes the preimage of
D_{\delta}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
under the blow-down map
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})\to N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
. The image of
{\mathbb{P}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})
is called the
exceptional divisor
.

According to the definition,
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}
admits a normal complex structure, and the blow-down map is a smooth map. If we merely have a suborbifold
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
such that
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
has a complex structure compatible with a given normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
, after choosing a Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{U}}
, we can deform the normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
to obtain a flattened normal complex structure so that we can perform the blow-up construction afterwards. Note that the space of auxiliary choices made in the construction is contractible. In particular,
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}
is well-defined up to isomorphism of orbifolds, and the resulting normal complex structures are all isotopic to each other. From now on, we will refer to
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
as
the
blow-up of
{\mathcal{U}}
along
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
, though the reader should keep in mind that certain choices need to be made.

Remark 3.6
.

Note that any open suborbifold
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
of a normally complex orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
acquires a natural normal complex structure. For a blow-up construction of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
whose center
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{\prime}
is the pullback of an suborbifold
{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{U}}
with complex normal bundle, we see that
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
is an open suborbifold of
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
provided that the flattened normal complex structures are compatible. When we state functoriality properties concerning resolutions of singularities under open embeddings, we always encounter this situation.

3.2.
The abelianization algorithm

For the statement of the next result, we say that an orbifold is
geometrically abelian
if the representation of the stabilizer associated to each point is a direct sum of
1
-dimensional representations. This is equivalent to the requirement that this stabilizer group be an extension of an abelian group by the stabilizer at a generic point.

Theorem 3.7

(Abelianization)
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{U}}
is a compact normally complex orbifold. Then there exists a geometrically abelian compact normally complex orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}^{ab}
with a smooth surjective map

(3.9)

\pi^{ab}:{\mathcal{U}}^{ab}\to{\mathcal{U}}

which is a composition of a sequence of blow-down maps such that for each blow-up, the center is a suborbifold with complex normal bundle.

Given an open embedding of compact normally complex orbifolds
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
, we have an open embedding of compact normally complex orbifolds
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{ab}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{ab}
such that the following diagram is commutative:

(3.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}_{2}^{ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{ab}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{ab}_{2}}

The rest of this subsection is devoted to a proof of this theorem. Throughout this section,
{\mathcal{U}}
stands for a compact normally complex orbifold.

Given a representative of an isotropy type
(\Gamma,V)
(cf. Definition
2.11
), decompose
V
into a direct sum of irreducible representations

(3.11)

V=\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{r}\oplus\tau_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\tau_{s},

where
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{r}
are
1
-dimensional complex
\Gamma
-representations and
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{s}
are complex
\Gamma
-representations of dimension
\geq 2
. The
1
-dimensional
\Gamma
-representations define characters

(3.12)

\chi_{i}:\Gamma\to{\mathbb{C}}^{*}

and we can define subgroup of
\Gamma

(3.13)

\overline{\Gamma}:=\bigcap_{i}\ker(\chi_{i}),

and construct a new isotropy type

(3.14)

(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{V}):=(\overline{\Gamma},\tau_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\tau_{s}).

It follows from the definition that the equivalence class of
(\overline{\Gamma},\overline{V})
is independent of the choice of the representative
(\Gamma,V)
.

Definition 3.8
.

Define the
non-abelianness
of an isotropy type
(\Gamma,V)
to be
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{V}
, and we denote it by
\mathrm{ord}^{na}(\Gamma,V)
.

For a normally complex orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, the non-abelianness function defines a map

(3.15)

\mathrm{ord}^{na}:|{\mathcal{U}}|\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0},

which takes
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
to the value of
\mathrm{ord}^{na}
at the normal invariant of
x
. We see that all the points in
{\mathcal{U}}
have stabilizer given by an extension of the generic stabilizer group by an abelian group if and only if
\mathrm{ord}^{na}\equiv 0
all over
|{\mathcal{U}}|
. In a nutshell, the proof of Theorem
3.7
is based on iteratively blowing up the loci along which
\mathrm{ord}^{na}
achieves its maximum.

Lemma 3.9
.

\mathrm{ord}^{na}:|{\mathcal{U}}|\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
is an upper semi-continuous function.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for a sequence
\{x_{\nu}\}\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|
with
x_{\nu}\to x
, we have

(3.16)

\mathrm{ord}^{na}(x)\geq\limsup_{\nu}\mathrm{ord}^{na}(x_{\nu}).

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
{\mathcal{U}}=\tilde{V}//\Gamma
such that:

•

\Gamma
is a finite group;

•

\tilde{V}
is a faithful
\Gamma
-representation with a decomposition
V={\mathbb{R}}^{k}\oplus V
, where
\Gamma
acts trivially on
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
and
V
is a complex
\Gamma
-representation without trivial summand;

•

x=0\in\tilde{V}
.

Then the stabilizer of any point
x_{\nu}
can be identified with a subgroup
\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}\leq\Gamma
. By restricting along
\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}
, the
\Gamma
-representation
V
can be decomposed as

(3.17)

V=\mathring{V}^{\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}}\oplus\check{V}^{\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}},

in which
\mathring{V}^{\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}}
is the trivial isotypic piece. Using the decomposition (
3.11
), we know that the direct sum of all irreducible
\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}
-representations of dimension
\geq 2
in
\check{V}^{\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}}
is a subspace of

(3.18)

(\tau_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\tau_{s})\cap\check{V}^{\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}}.

Because the normal invariant of
x_{\nu}
is represented by
(\Gamma_{x_{\nu}},\check{V}^{\Gamma_{x_{\nu}}})
, we see that
\mathrm{ord}^{na}(x_{\nu})\leq\mathrm{ord}^{na}(x)
and the assertion follows.
∎

Denote by
|{\mathcal{U}}_{na}|
the subspace of
|{\mathcal{U}}|
consists of all the points at which
\mathrm{ord}^{na}
achieves its maximum, then we immediately have

Corollary 3.10
.

|{\mathcal{U}}_{na}|
is a closed subset of
|{\mathcal{U}}|
. ∎

In fact, we can look at more refined structures.

Lemma 3.11
.

The closed substack
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
which is defined to be the preimage of
|{\mathcal{U}}_{na}|
of the coarse space map
{\mathcal{U}}\to|{\mathcal{U}}|
is a suborbifold, which can be equipped with complex normal bundle.

Proof.

We make a preliminary observation: given an isotropy type represented by
(\Gamma,V)
, then the subgroup
\overline{\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
appearing in Equation (
3.14
), is
normal
.

Let
\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be an étale atlas which equips
{\mathcal{U}}
with a normal complex structure as in Definition
2.9
. Based on Proposition
2.3
, passing to a refinement if necessary, we can assume that
U_{\alpha}
is an open subset of an
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear faithful
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-representation
\tilde{V}_{\alpha}
such that
\tilde{V}_{\alpha}={\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}\oplus V_{\alpha}
, where
{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}
is the trivial isotypic piece and
V_{\alpha}
is a complex
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-representation without any trivial summand.

Suppose that
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}_{na}|
is contained in the image of
U_{\alpha}/\Gamma_{\alpha}
. Up to a further refinement, we can assume that all such étale charts are
centered
at
x
, i.e.
0\in U_{\alpha}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}\oplus V_{\alpha}
and
\Gamma_{\alpha}
is isomorphic to
\Gamma_{x}
. Using the subgroup
\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}\leq\Gamma_{\alpha}
(cf. (
3.13
)), we obtain a decomposition

(3.19)

V_{\alpha}=\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}\oplus\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}},

in which
\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
is the trivial isotypic piece of the restricted
\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}
-representation. Then we know that
|{\mathcal{U}}_{na}|
is covered by the union of images of

(3.20)

\big(U_{\alpha}\cap({\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}\oplus\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})\big)/\Gamma_{\alpha},

and any point in it is mapped into
|{\mathcal{U}}_{na}|
. Note that
\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
is preserved by
\Gamma_{\alpha}
because
\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}\leq\Gamma_{\alpha}
is a normal subgroup. The disjoint union

(3.21)

\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}\cap({\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}\oplus\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})

with
({\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}/\Gamma_{\alpha})\cap|{\mathcal{U}}_{na}|\neq\emptyset
defines a smooth étale atlas of the closed substack
{\mathcal{U}}_{\alpha}
. Indeed, the compatibility is already encoded in the orbifold structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
visualized through
\coprod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}\to{\mathcal{U}}
. By construction, we see that
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is a suborbifold.

Finally, using the decomposition in Equation (
3.19
), the complex structure on
\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
, as part of the normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
, endows the normal bundle
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
with a complex structure.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.

If the function
\mathrm{ord}^{na}
identically vanishes on
{\mathcal{U}}
, the orbifold is already geometrically abelian, so we may assume that the maximally non-abelian locus
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
as in Lemma
3.11
is non-empty.

Blow up
{\mathcal{U}}
along
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
to obtain
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{na}}{\mathcal{U}}
. Then we claim that

(3.22)

\max_{|\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{na}}{\mathcal{U}}|}\mathrm{ord}^{na}<\max_{|{\mathcal{U}}|}\mathrm{ord}^{na}.

Because the blow-down map
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{na}}{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is an isomorphism away from
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
, by the maximality of
\mathrm{ord}^{na}
along
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
, we only need to verify the above inequality over the exceptional divisor
{\mathbb{P}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{na})
. It suffices to check things étale locally. Using the notations from (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), we can locally identify
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
as

(3.23)

({\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}\oplus\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})//\Gamma_{\alpha}\subset V_{\alpha}//\Gamma_{\alpha}.

Then the exceptional divisor
{\mathbb{P}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{na})
can be locally identified with

(3.24)

\big(({\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}\oplus\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})\times{\mathbb{P}}(\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})\big)//\Gamma_{\alpha},

where
\Gamma_{\alpha}
acts on
{\mathbb{P}}(\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})
as projective linear transformations. Then
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{na}}{\mathcal{U}}
can be locally identified with the global finite quotient orbifold

(3.25)

\big(({\mathbb{R}}^{k_{\alpha}}\oplus\mathring{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{P}(\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})}(-1)\big)//\Gamma_{\alpha},

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{P}(\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})}(-1)\to{\mathbb{P}}(\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})
is the tautological line bundle with the natural
\Gamma_{\alpha}
-action. Note that the space of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{P}(\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})}(-1)\setminus\mathbb{P}(\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}})
can be identified with the complement of the origin of
\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
. On the other hand, the normal invariant of any point in the coarse space of the exceptional divisor is equal to the normal invariant of any nonzero point (in the coarse space) lying on the line specified by such a point in the projective space.
For any point in the coarse space of the left side of (
3.23
), we know the value of
\mathrm{ord}^{na}
is equal to
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
. On the other hand, the isotropy group of any vector in
\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}\setminus\{0\}
has a nontrivial invariant subspace containing the span of this vector in
\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
, which implies that the value of
\mathrm{ord}^{na}
at any point in the exceptional divisor is strictly less than
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\check{V}_{\alpha}^{\overline{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}
. As a result,
\max\mathrm{ord}^{na}
gets decreased under the blow-up.

Due to the compactness of
{\mathcal{U}}
, after blowing up along
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
for finitely many times, we can guarantee that
\mathrm{ord}^{na}\equiv 0
. This final output is defined to be
{\mathcal{U}}^{ab}
, and the composition of blow-down maps defines the map
\pi^{ab}:{\mathcal{U}}^{ab}\to{\mathcal{U}}
satisfying all the requirements. Moreover, it follows from the construction that abelianization is functorial under open embeddings.
∎

3.3.
Discussions

Due to the elementary nature of the abelianization procedure, it deserves some further comments to make connections with results from the literature. The reader is invited to figure out methods to abelianize normally complex orbifolds.

Our choice of invariant, which follows
[
BR19
]
, is referred to as the
codimension of stackiness
in
loc. cit.
. Alternatively, as pointed to the authors by David Rydh, one could use the order of
\overline{\Gamma}
as an invariant.

If
{\mathcal{U}}
is the finite global quotient orbifold constructed from a complex manifold acted on complex analytically by a finite group, there is an alternative construction due to Batyrev
[
Bat00
]
. There, the invariant measuring the ‘nonabelian-ness’ is simply the maximal value of orders of non-abelian groups appearing as stabilizers of
{\mathcal{U}}
. There is a variant of
{\mathcal{U}}_{na}
in this setting, which is constructed from the fixed point locus of
\overline{\Gamma}
if
\Gamma
achieves the maximum of the ‘nonabelian-ness’ invariant here. However, because there may be multiple choices of
\Gamma
, the union of the corresponding fixed point loci of
\overline{\Gamma}
may not be a smooth suborbifold. In the algebraic or complex analytic setting, this union is a subvariety with simple normal crossings. The crossings need to be removed using blow-ups before performing other blow-ups to reduce the ‘nonabelian-ness’. It should be possible to adapt a variant of Batyrev’s algorithm in our setting, though the technicalities to be deployed may be more substantial.

As observed in
[
RY00
, Theorem 4.1]
, the functorial resolution in characteristic
0
of Bierstone–Milman
[
BM97
]
(or other approaches which make Hironaka’s original method functorial) can be used to abelianize smooth Deligne–Mumford stacks over
{\mathbb{C}}
(we learned this from
[
Ber17
]
). The idea is based on performing blow-ups such that any point with nontrivial stabilizer is contained in a (locally invariant) simple normal crossings divisor. If this is the case, the action of the stabilizer on the normal space of the divisor factors through
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{k}
. Such a method may also work in the almost complex category, but it has the disadvantage that the resolution procedure is rather implicit.

4.
Desingularizing the coarse spaces

In this section, we follow Bergh’s functorial destackification algorithm of Deligne–Mumford stacks with abelian stabilizers
[
Ber17
]
to perform blow-up and root stack constructions to obtain from any compact and geometrically abelian normally complex orbifold, a compact and effective normally complex orbifold whose coarse space is a smooth manifold. As in the abelianization procedure, the fact that the algebro-geometric approach in
[
Ber17
]
can be applied to study normally complex orbifolds is eventually due to the fact that all the invariants which specify the center of blow-up or the divisor along which the root stack is taken are derived from the normal invariant Definition
2.13
, and these centers are suborbifolds with complex normal bundle.

A minor difference with the setup in
[
Ber17
]
is that we do not assume effectiveness of the underlying orbifold. In our presentation, for a finite group
\Gamma
, we will use the same symbol to denote a
1
-dimensional complex
\Gamma
-representation and its associated character.

4.1.
Root stacks

We begin by defining the
root stack
procedure to incorporate ideas of
stacky blow-ups
from, e.g.,
[
Ber17
,
BR19
]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{U}}
is a normally complex orbifold. A real codimension
2
suborbifold
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
is called a
divisor
if
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{D}}
admits a complex structure which is compatible with the normal complex structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
(cf. Definition
3.4
).

A familiar construction from, e.g.,
[
GH78
, Section 1.1]
, shows that given a divisor
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
, there exists a complex line bundle
L_{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
characterized by:

(1)

L_{\mathcal{D}}
is isomorphic to the product bundle over
{\mathcal{U}}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}
;

(2)

identifying an open neighborhood of
{\mathcal{D}}\subset N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{D}}
with an open subset in
{\mathcal{U}}
containing
{\mathcal{D}}
, the restriction of
L_{\mathcal{D}}
to such an open subset is isomorphic to the pullback of
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{D}}
under the projection
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
.

Moreover, there exists a section
\tau_{\mathcal{D}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to L_{\mathcal{D}}
whose vanishing locus is exactly
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Definition 4.2
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{D}}
is a divisor in a normally complex orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
. Let
L_{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be the associated line bundle and write
L_{\mathcal{D}}^{\times}
for the complement of the zero-section. Consider the bundle map

(4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathcal{D}}^{\times}\times{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}

defined by mapping
(l,c)\in L_{\mathcal{D}}^{\times}\times{\mathbb{C}}
to
c^{d}l
, where
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
. Denote the preimage of the embedding
\tau_{D}:{\mathcal{U}}\to L_{\mathcal{D}}
in
L_{\mathcal{D}}^{\times}\times{\mathbb{C}}
by
V(\tau_{\mathcal{D}})
. Then the
d
-th root stack
of
{\mathcal{U}}
along
{\mathcal{D}}
is defined to be the quotient orbifold

(4.2)

V(\tau_{\mathcal{D}})/{\mathbb{C}}^{*},

where the group
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
acts on
V(\tau_{\mathcal{D}})
by restricting the
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
-action on
L_{\mathcal{D}}^{\times}\times{\mathbb{C}}
given by

(4.3)

(z,(l,c))\to(z^{-d}\cdot l,z\cdot c),\quad\quad\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}.

Remark 4.3
.

Our construction of root stacks is simply an implementation of the construction from algebraic geometry, see, e.g.,
[
AGV08
, Appendix B]
.

4.2.
Statements and definitions

We now state the main theorem of this section. Then we introduce fundamental definitions and a collection of invariants which measure how far the coarse space of
{\mathcal{U}}
is being smooth.

Definition 4.4
.

A
normally complex pair

({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
consists of:

(1)

a compact normally complex
geometrically abelian
orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
;

(2)

an ordered collection
\mathbf{D}=\{{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{D}}_{r}\}
of divisors of
{\mathcal{U}}
which intersect transversely, such that for any
x\in|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|
, the action of
\Gamma_{x}
on the corresponding fiber of
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is nontrivial.

This is the analogue of
standard pair
from
[
Ber17
, Definition 2.1]
. The requirement (2) is the counterpart of simple normal crossings divisors in our context, which we have strengthened by requiring the non-triviality of the action of stabilizer groups,
since a divisor whose normal bundle carries the trivial representation can be neglected in our construction.
Note that the components
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
are not assumed to be connected, and it is important to keep track of the orderings. For two normally complex pairs
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})
and
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
, we use the notation
f:({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})\to({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
to denote a smooth map
f:{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}
which maps
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
into
\mathbf{D}
such that the orderings are respected.

Definition 4.5

(c.f.
[
Ber17
, Definition 2.7]
)
.

A normally complex pair with
distinguished structure
is a triple
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D},\acute{\mathbf{D}})
such that
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is a normally complex pair,
\acute{\mathbf{D}}\subset\mathbf{D}
is a subcollection of divisors from
\mathbf{D}
which are not preceded, under the ordering of
\mathbf{D}
, by any element of
\mathbf{D}\setminus\acute{\mathbf{D}}
.

The notation
f:({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})\to({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D},\acute{\mathbf{D}})
should be self-explanatory based on the previous definition.

Theorem 4.6
.

If
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is a normally complex pair, then there exists a sequence of normally complex pairs

(4.4)

\pi^{M}:(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\tilde{\mathbf{D}}):=({\mathcal{U}}_{n},\mathbf{D}_{n})\xrightarrow{\pi_{n}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}({\mathcal{U}}_{0},\mathbf{D}_{0})=({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})

such that:

(1)

each
\pi_{i}:{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{i-1}
is a smooth map induced from either the blow-up along a suborbifold with complex normal bundle in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i-1}
, or a root stack construction along a divisor in
\mathbf{D}_{i-1}
;

(2)

the coarse space
|\tilde{\mathcal{U}}|
is a smooth
manifold
.

If
f:({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})\to({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
defines an open suborbifold
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
such that
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}=f^{*}\mathbf{D}
and the normal complex structures are compatible, then there exists a smooth map
\tilde{f}:(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})\to(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\tilde{\mathbf{D}})
defining an open suborbifold
\tilde{f}:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
such that the commutative diagram

(4.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\tilde{\mathbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is Cartesian. Accordingly, by passing to coarse space, we obtain a smooth embedding

(4.6)

|\tilde{f}|:|\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}|\to|\tilde{\mathcal{U}}|.

The above construction is referred to as a
deorbification
process. If we start with
\mathbf{D}=\emptyset
, it provides a method to obtain an orbifold with smooth coarse space from an normally complex orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
with only abelian geometric stabilizers. Although the divisors could be eliminated in the final statement which asserts the smoothness of
|\tilde{\mathcal{U}}|
, they play a pivotal role in the proof. As can be inferred from the statement, such a deorbification is functorial in under open embeddings. This functoriality is important for the compatibility between local and global constructions. We will prove Theorem
4.6
at the end of this section after finishing all the preparatory work.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
be a normally complex pair. Given
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
, let
(\Gamma,V)
be a representative of its normal invariant (cf. Definition
2.13
). For
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
, define

(4.7)

\chi_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \text{the character associated to $N_{{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}{\mathcal{U}}$}\subset V,\quad\quad\quad&\text{if }x\in|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|,\\
0\quad\quad\quad&\text{if }x\notin|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|.\end{aligned}\right.

The characters
\{\chi_{i}\}
inherits an ordering from the ordering of the divisors. If
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V=s
, we can decompose it into a direct sum of irreducible representations

(4.8)

V\cong\chi_{i_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}\oplus\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s}=\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s},

in which the representations/characters
\chi_{i_{1}},\dots\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}
come from the normal directions of the divisors and
\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}},\dots,\chi_{s}
are the ‘residual’ directions.

All of our subsequent invariants are constructed out of the decomposition of the normal invariant (
4.8
). Given a finite group
\Gamma
, we use the notation
\chi(\Gamma)
to denote the group of characters of
\Gamma
, which is also known as the
Cartier dual
. For a sequence of characters
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{l}
, write
\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{l}\rangle
for the subgroup of
\chi(\Gamma)
generated by these characters. Using this notation, define the subgroups of
\chi(\Gamma)

(4.9)

\chi(x):=\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle,\quad\quad\quad\chi_{div}(x):=\langle\chi_{i_{1}},\dots,\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}\rangle

following (
4.8
). Concretely,
\chi_{div}(x)
is the group of
\Gamma
-characters generated by those associated to the normal representations of the divisors in
\mathbf{D}
passing through
x
.

Definition 4.7

(
[
Ber17
, Definition 7.1]
)
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
be a normally complex pair. Let
(\Gamma,V)
be a representative of the normal invariant of
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
with decomposition (
4.8
). The
independence index

of
x
is defined to be the number of indices
i\in\{1,\dots,s\}
such that

(4.10)

\langle\chi_{i}\rangle\cap\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\hat{\chi_{i}},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle=\{0\}.

Note that if the independence index of
x
is
0
, the coarse space
|{\mathcal{U}}|
is naturally a smooth manifold near
x
because locally the coarse space, which is a quotient space, is homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V
. A trivial but illustrative example is the following: for the cyclic group
C_{2}
of order
2
, let
{\mathbb{C}}(0)
be the trivial representation and
{\mathbb{C}}(-1)
be the sign representation. Then the coarse space
|({\mathbb{C}}(0)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}(-1))//C_{2}|
is smooth, but
|({\mathbb{C}}(-1)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}(-1))//C_{2}|
is not smooth. The independence index does not use any information from
\mathbf{D}
.

Definition 4.8

(
[
Ber17
, Definition 7.4]
)
.

Suppose
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is a normally complex pair. Let
(\Gamma,V)
be a representative of the normal invariant of
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
with decomposition (
4.8
). The
toroidal index
of
x
is defined to be the number
s-r^{\prime}
.

If the toroidal index of
x
is
0
, then the
\Gamma
-representation
V
is decomposed into the direct sum of the normal representations from divisors in
\mathbf{D}
passing through
x
. If this is the case,
x
admits an orbifold chart modeled on
toric orbifolds
along the normal directions, whose partial deorbification process will be recalled in Section
4.3
. Reducing the local model to toric orbifolds allows us to use combinatorial methods to achieve the deorbification/destackification.

Abusing the notation, write
\mathbf{D}
the totally ordered set consisting of the indices of the divisors in
\mathbf{D}
. For a normally complex pair
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
and
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
, denote by
\chi(x)^{\mathbf{D}}
the set of
|\mathbf{D}|
-tuples of characters in the group
\chi(x)
, where each component which comes from the normal representation of a divisor in
\mathbf{D}
should be thought of as marked by the corresponding index.

Definition 4.9

(
[
Ber17
, Definition 7.8]
)
.

Given a point
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
as above with decomposition (
4.8
), define the tuple
\mathbf{v}(x)\in\chi(x)^{\mathbf{D}}
by

(4.11)

\mathbf{v}(x)_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} 0\quad\quad\quad&\text{if }x\notin|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|\text{ or }x\in|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|\text{ and }\langle\chi_{i}\rangle\cap\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\hat{\chi_{i}},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle=\{0\},\\
\chi_{i},\quad\quad\quad&\text{if }x\in|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|\text{ and }\langle\chi_{i}\rangle\subset\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\hat{\chi_{i}},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

where
\chi_{i}
is the character associated to
N_{{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}{\mathcal{U}}
. Denote by
A(x)
the subgroup of
\chi(x)
generated by characters in
\mathbf{v}(x)
. Then the
divisorial type
of
x
is defined to be the pair

(4.12)

(A(x),\mathbf{v}(x)).

Note that all the characters in
\mathbf{v}(x)
are marked by a divisor from
\mathbf{D}
. We can introduce a well-ordering on the set of divisorial types of all points in
|{\mathcal{U}}|
. Let
j_{1},\dots,j_{m}
be all the indices in
\mathbf{D}
such that
\mathbf{v}(x)_{j_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{v}(x)_{j_{m}}\neq 0
, and we assume that
j_{1},\dots,j_{m}
is in the decreasing order. Then we have a short exact sequence of abelian groups

(4.13)

0\to K(x)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}\to A(x)\to 0

where
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}\to A(x)
is defined by
\mathbf{v}(x)_{i}\mapsto\chi_{i}
and
K(x)
is the kernel. Because
A(x)
is a finite group,
K(x)
is a free
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module of rank
m
. Then we can write the map
K(x)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
as a matrix with integer entries
H=(a_{ij})_{m\times m}
using the Hermite normal form, which is characterized by:

(1)

H
is upper triangular;

(2)

a_{ii}>0
, and
a_{ii}>a_{ij}\geq 0
for all
j>i
.

Using the ordering on
\mathbf{D}
, the order on the set of divisorial types is induced from the lexicographic order on the set of corresponding
H
: here we first look at the lexicographic order of the sequence
(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})
, then the lexicographic order on entries of
H
, where the entries are first ordered by rows, with high row numbers being more significant, then ordered by columns, with lower column numbers being more significant. Such a well-order refines the following partial order on the set of divisorial types:
(A(x),\mathbf{v}(x))\geq(A(x^{\prime}),\mathbf{v}(x^{\prime}))
if there exists a surjective group homomorphism
A(x)\to A(x^{\prime})
taking
\mathbf{v}(x)
to
\mathbf{v}(x^{\prime})
. The reader should consult
[
Ber17
, Section 7]
for more explanations and illustrations through examples.

Definition 4.10

(
[
Ber17
, Definition 7.13]
)
.

Given a normally complex pair
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
, let
(\Gamma,V)
be a representative of the isotropy type of
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
. Using the decomposition (
4.8
), define the
divisorial index
of
x
to be the number of characters from
\{\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}},\dots,\chi_{s}\}
specified in (
4.8
) which does not lie in
\chi_{div}(x)
from (
4.9
).
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is said to be
divisorial
at
x
if the divisorial index of
x
is
0
.

Remark 4.11
.

If
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is divisorial at all the points of
|{\mathcal{U}}|
, it has the following feature. For any
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\in\mathbf{D}
, let
L_{{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}
be the dual line bundle and denote by
P^{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}
the associated principal
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
-bundle. Then the fiber product

(4.14)

P^{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}\times_{\mathcal{U}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{U}}P^{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{r}}

has pure isotropy, so that the coarse space is actually a
smooth manifold
. It suffices to check this near each
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
, where the statement reduces to the claim that, in the effective case, the isotropy is trivial. Indeed, because
\chi_{div}(x)=\chi(x)
at any
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
, the action of
\Gamma_{x}=\Gamma
on the direct sum of the normal representations of the divisors in
\mathbf{D}
passing through
x
, which is
\chi_{i_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}
from (
4.8
), is
faithful
due to the effectiveness assumption of
{\mathcal{U}}
. As a result, the induced group homomorphism
\Gamma_{x}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{r}
, which is defined to be the product of
\Gamma_{x}
-representation which is the fiber of
L_{{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}
over
x
, is injective. In other words, the direct sum
L_{{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{{\mathcal{D}}_{r}}
is a
faithful vector bundle
is the sense of
[
Par22
, Remark 1.6]
.

Definition 4.12

(
[
Ber17
, Definition 7.16]
)
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
be a normally complex pair, and let
x
be a point at which it is divisorial, with decomposition of its normal invariant as in Equation (
4.8
).

If
x
lies on a divisor
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathbf{D}
, define the subgroup
\chi_{div}(x)^{\mathcal{D}}
of
\chi_{div}(x)
(cf. Equation (
4.9
)) to be generated by all the characters from
\{\chi_{i_{1}},\dots,\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}\}
except the one corresponding to
{\mathcal{D}}
. Write

(4.15)

\chi_{\mathcal{D}}(x):=\chi_{div}(x)/\chi_{div}(x)^{\mathcal{D}},

which is a cyclic group. Because all the characters from Equation (
4.8
) are contained in
\chi_{div}(x)
, for any character
\chi_{i}
from
\{\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}},\dots,\chi_{s}\}
, there exists a nonnegative integer
c_{i}
such that the image of
\chi_{i}
under the projection

(4.16)

\chi(x)=\chi_{div}(x)\to\chi_{\mathcal{D}}(x)

is
c_{i}
times the generator. Then the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index
at
x
is defined to be

(4.17)

\sum_{i=i_{r^{\prime}}+1}^{s}c_{i}.

If
x\notin|{\mathcal{D}}|
, its
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index is defined to be
0
.

Continuing using the notations as above, if for any
\chi_{i}\in\{\chi_{i_{1}},\dots,\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}\}
we have

(4.18)

\langle\chi_{i}\rangle\cap\langle\chi_{i_{1}},\dots,\hat{\chi_{i}},\dots\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}\rangle=\{0\},

one can see that if the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index vanishes at
x
for all
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathbf{D}
, the so does the independence index from Definition
4.7
. Accordingly, in this situation, the coarse space
|{\mathcal{U}}|
is smooth near
x
.

We will make use of the invariants described in this subsection to achieve the functorial deorbification Theorem
4.6
step by step.

4.3.
Toric local models

Suppose that
(\Gamma,V)
is a representative of an isotropy type such that
V
is decomposed as

(4.19)

V=\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s}

in which each
\chi_{i}
is a
1
-dimensional complex
\Gamma
-representation, and we use the same notation to denote the associated
\Gamma
-character. The goal of this subsection is to extend
[
Ber17
, Algorithm A]
, which defines a “partial” resolution in the case
\Gamma
is abelian to our setting. We begin by recalling the basic notions of toric orbifolds:

Definition 4.13
.

An
orbifold fan
is a triple
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(N,\Sigma,\beta)
where
N
is a finite rank free abelian group,
\Sigma
is a simplicial fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}=N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}
, and
\beta:{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma(1)}\to N
is a group homomorphism from the free abelian group generated by rays in
\Sigma
which maps each generator
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
to a non-zero lattice point on the ray
\rho
.

Any orbifold fan gives rise to a toric orbifold
X_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
:
\beta
induces an exact sequence

(4.20)

1\to\Delta(\Sigma)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{*}\to N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{*}\to 1.

One way to express
X_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
is to start with affine space
\mathbb{C}^{\Sigma(1)}
, on which
{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma(1)}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts, remove the unstable locus
Z(\Sigma)
with respect to the action of
\Delta(\Sigma)
, and take the corresponding quotient for this group.

In the special case of a
\Gamma
-representation
V
equipped with a decomposition as in Equation (
4.19
), if we write
\chi(V):=\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle\subset\chi(\Gamma)
, we have a group homomorphism

(4.21)

{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}\to\chi(V)

whose kernel
K
is free of rank
s
. By applying the dual
\hom(-,{\mathbb{Z}})
to the short exact sequence
0\to K\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}\to\chi(V)\to 0
, we obtain a homomorphism

(4.22)

\beta:\hom({\mathbb{Z}}^{s},{\mathbb{Z}})\to\hom(K,{\mathbb{Z}}).

Now take
N=\hom(K,{\mathbb{Z}})
and let
\Sigma\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}
be the fan associated to the cone corresponding to the ‘first quadrant’. In this case, we have that the group
\Delta(\Sigma)
in Equation (
4.20
) is exactly the image of
\Gamma
in the torus. The Cox construction of such an orbifold fan recovers the orbifold quotient of
V
by the image of
\Gamma
.

Toric orbifolds with an ordering on toric divisors are examples of normally complex pairs. Given an ordering on the rays of an orbifold fan, one can define a distinguished structure as in Definition
4.5
for a collection of toric divisors.

If
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(N,\Sigma,\beta)
is an orbifold fan, for a cone
\sigma\subset\Sigma
, define
v_{\sigma}=\sum_{\rho\in\sigma(1)}\beta(\rho)\in N_{\mathbb{R}}
and let
\rho_{v_{\sigma}}
be the ray generated by
v_{\sigma}
. Then the
star subdivision
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
along
\sigma
is the orbifold fan
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{*}(\sigma)=(N,\Sigma^{*}(\sigma),\beta^{*})
where
\Sigma^{*}(\sigma)
is obtained from
\Sigma
by subdivision using
v_{\sigma}
, and
\beta^{*}
extends
\beta
by additionally mapping the generator associated to
\rho_{v_{\sigma}}
to
v_{\sigma}
. This is the combinatorial counterpart of blow-up, as familiar from toric blow-ups of toric varieties.

There is also a reformulation of taking root stacks using orbifold fans. Namely, given an orbifold fan
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(N,\Sigma,\beta)
and a map
\mathbf{d}:\Sigma(1)\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, we can define a homomorphism
\beta^{\prime}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma(1)}\to N
with
\beta^{\prime}(\rho)=\mathbf{d}(\rho)\beta(\rho)
. Then we obtain a new orbifold fan
\Sigma_{\mathbf{d}^{-1}\rho}:=(N,\Sigma,\beta^{\prime})
. It is straightforward to see that the corresponding toric orbifold is constructed by iteratively taking
\mathbf{d}(\rho)
-th root stack along the toric divisor specified by
\rho\in\Sigma(1)
.

Proposition 4.14

(
[
Ber17
, Algorithm A]
)
.

Given an orbifold fan
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{0}
with an ordered set of rays having a distinguished structure, there exists a sequence

(4.23)

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{n}\to\cdots\to\mathbf{\Sigma}_{0}

of toric fans such that for any
0\leq i\leq n-1
, the orbifold fan
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i+1}
is obtained from
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i}
by a star subdivision or a root stack construction. Moreover, the following properties hold.

(1)

Each orbifold fan
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{i}
has an ordered set of rays with a distinguished structure.

(2)

for
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{n}
, let
(X_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{n}},\mathbf{D}_{n},\acute{\mathbf{D}}_{n})
be the associated normally complex pair with a distinguished structure. Then for any
x\in|X_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{n}}|
, if the normal invariant of
x
is represented by
(\Gamma,V)
with decomposition (
4.8
), then (
4.10
) holds for any
\chi_{i}
from
N_{X_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{n}}}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
with
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\in\acute{\mathbf{D}}_{n}
.

The content of the above statement can be summarized as “making the distinguished divisors independent”. We refer the reader to the original reference for full proof. We remark that our definition of normally complex pairs requires the ambient orbifold to be compact, which is not necessarily satisfied by
X_{\mathbf{\Sigma}_{n}}
, but we hope that the meaning of the above statement is transparent.

We shall use the above result in Section
4.6
to lower the independence the independence index of a possibly ineffective orbifold whose underlying effective orbifold is abelian, as all the steps in the construction of Proposition
4.14
lift to the ineffective case.

Remark 4.15

(
[
Ber17
, Remark 7.11]
)
.

In the case of Definition
4.9
, for each divisorial type
(A(x),\mathbf{v}(x))
, using the short exact sequence (
4.13
), we can define an orbifold fan
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(N,\Sigma,\beta)
with
N=\hom(K(x),{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\beta
being the dual of
K(x)\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
. This orbifold fan has a unique maximal cone, and the rays in
\Sigma(1)
are labeled by the corresponding indices in
\mathbf{D}
.

4.4.
Divisorialification

We establish the following analogue of
[
Ber17
, Algorithm C]
for normally complex pairs.

Proposition 4.16
.

If
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is a normally complex pair then there exists a sequence of normally complex pairs

(4.24)

({\mathcal{U}}^{div},\mathbf{D}^{div}):=({\mathcal{U}}_{n},\mathbf{D}_{n})\xrightarrow{\pi_{n}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}({\mathcal{U}}_{0},\mathbf{D}_{0})=({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})

such that:

(1)

each
\pi_{i}:{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{i-1}
is a smooth map induced from the blow-up along a suborbifold with complex normal bundle in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i-1}
;

(2)

for any point
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}_{n}|
, the divisorial index (cf. Definition
4.10
) at
x
defined using the pair
({\mathcal{U}}_{n},\mathbf{D}_{n})
is
0
.

If
f:({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})\to({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is an open embedding of a suborbifold
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
such that
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}=f^{*}\mathbf{D}
and the normal complex structures are compatible, then there exists a smooth map
\tilde{f}:(({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})^{div},(\mathbf{D}^{\prime})^{div})\to({\mathcal{U}}^{div},\mathbf{D}^{div})
which defines an open embedding of orbifolds
\tilde{f}:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
with compatible normal complex structures such that the commutative diagram

(4.25)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})^{div},(\mathbf{D}^{\prime})^{div})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}}^{div},\mathbf{D}^{div})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is Cartesian.

Note that we only need to blow up along suborbifolds with complex normal bundle to achieve divisorialification. The construction is based on blowing up along the locus at which the divisorial index from Definition
4.10
achieves its maximum. The following is a necessary preliminary result.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
be a normally complex pair. Let
|{\mathcal{U}}_{div}|\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|
be the subset which consists of points at which the divisorial index achieves its maximum. Then
|{\mathcal{U}}_{div}|
is a closed subset of
|{\mathcal{U}}|
, and the closed substack
{\mathcal{U}}_{div}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
which is defined to be the preimage of
|{\mathcal{U}}_{div}|
under the coarse space map
{\mathcal{U}}\to|{\mathcal{U}}|
is a suborbifold which is equipped with complex normal bundle.

Proof.

If all the points in
|{\mathcal{U}}|
have divisorial index
0
, we have
{\mathcal{U}}_{div}={\mathcal{U}}
and there is nothing to prove. So let us assume that there is at least one point in
|{\mathcal{U}}|
at which the divisorial index function is nonzero.

To show that
|{\mathcal{U}}_{div}|
is closed, it suffices to show that the divisorial index function is a upper semi-continuous function on
|{\mathcal{U}}|
. To this end, let
\{x_{\nu}\}\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|
be a sequence such that
x_{\nu}\to x
. Suppose the normal invariant of
x
is represented by
(\Gamma,V)
which comes with the decomposition (
4.8
). Without loss of generality, we can assume that
x
is covered by an orbifold chart
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V
such that
\Gamma
acts on
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
trivially. Note that it suffices to figure out the argument for
k=0
which we assume from now on. Let the divisorial index of
x
be
d
and suppose that it is realized by characters
\chi_{s-d+1},\dots,\chi_{s}
. By assumption, over the orbifold chart, we can identify the preimages of the divisors
{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{1}},\dots,{\mathcal{D}}_{i_{r^{\prime}}}
with the complex codimension
1
subspaces
V/\chi_{i_{1}},\dots,V/\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}
. Here the quotient should be understood as taking the complement. For
x_{\nu}=x^{\prime}
, we can assume that
\Gamma_{x^{\prime}}=\Gamma^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
. The interesting case is when
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a proper subgroup of
\Gamma
, otherwise the divisorial index of
x_{\nu}
agrees with that of
x
, so let us assume
\Gamma^{\prime}<\Gamma
. Denote the induced decomposition (
4.8
) of the restriction of
V
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
by

(4.26)

\chi_{i_{1}}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}^{\prime}\oplus\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s}^{\prime}.

Then observe that

(4.27)

\chi_{div}(x^{\prime})=\langle\chi_{i_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}^{\prime}\rangle.

Consequently, the characters which are not contained in
\chi_{div}(x^{\prime})
form a subset of
\{\chi_{s-d+1}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}\}
, so we see that the divisorial index of
x^{\prime}
is bounded from above by that of
x
. Therefore, the divisorial index function is upper semi-continuous.

Next we investigate the closed substack
{\mathcal{U}}_{div}
. Using the notations introduced in the previous paragraph, we see that for
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}_{div}|
covered by the orbifold chart
({\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V)//\Gamma
, the preimage of
|{\mathcal{U}}_{div}|
is locally given by

(4.28)

{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times(\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s-d}),

which is preserved by the
\Gamma
-action. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
3.11
, we see that we can use these orbifold charts to endow
{\mathcal{U}}_{div}\to{\mathcal{U}}
with a suborbifold structure because any subgroup of an abelian group is normal. The existence of complex structure on its normal bundle is manifest, as the
\Gamma
-representation
\chi_{s-d+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s}
is complex.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.16
.

Given a normally complex pair
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
, if the divisorial index is constantly zero, the construction terminates.

Otherwise, we consider the effective and compact normally complex orbifold
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{div}}{\mathcal{U}}
with divisors
\hat{\mathbf{D}}=\mathbf{D}\cup{\mathbb{P}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{div})
, in which
\mathbf{D}
is the strict transform and the last component denotes the exceptional divisor that is endowed with an order which is greater than all other components from
\mathbf{D}
. Note that
(\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{div}}{\mathcal{U}},\hat{\mathbf{D}})
is still a normally complex pair, which can be checked by looking at the local picture as exhibited in Lemma
4.17
.

We wish to show that the maximum of the divisorial index for
(\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{div}}{\mathcal{U}},\hat{\mathbf{D}})
is strictly smaller than that of the pair
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
. If this is the case, due to the compactness of
{\mathcal{U}}
, we just need finitely many steps to obtain
({\mathcal{U}}^{div},\mathbf{D}^{div})
. Note that this construction is automatically functorial in the sense of the statement of Proposition
4.16
: the order between divisorial indices is preserved by restricting to open suborbifolds and the blow-up is functorial for inclusion of open suborbifolds.

To finish the proof, we just need to check that for any
\hat{x}\in|\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{div}}{\mathcal{U}}|
on the exceptional divisor
|{\mathbb{P}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{div})|
, the divisorial index of
\hat{x}
is strictly smaller than the divisorial index of its image
x
under the blow-down map. Let’s use the notations in the proof of Lemma
4.17
to construct an orbifold chart
({\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V)//\Gamma
covering
x
. Then near
\hat{x}
, we can find a finite quotient orbifold embedded as a suborbifold of
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{div}}{\mathcal{U}}
of the form

(4.29)

\big({\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times(\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s-d})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}(\chi_{s-d+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\chi_{s})}(-1)\big)//\Gamma

as exhibited in the proof of Theorem
3.7
. For
\hat{x}
, its divisorial index must be bounded from above by
d-1
because the normal direction of the exceptional divisor is certainly contained in
\chi_{div}(\hat{x})
.
∎

4.5.
Annihilating
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index

The aim here is to establish a counterpart of
[
Ber17
, Algorithm D]
in the smooth category.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D},\acute{\mathbf{D}})
be a normally complex pair with distinguished structure in the sense of Definition
4.5
. Then there exists a sequence of normally complex pairs with distinguished structure

(4.30)

({\mathcal{U}}^{dis},\mathbf{D}^{dis},\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{dis}):=({\mathcal{U}}_{n},\mathbf{D}_{n},\acute{\mathbf{D}}_{n})\xrightarrow{\pi_{n}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}({\mathcal{U}}_{0},\mathbf{D}_{0},\acute{\mathbf{D}}_{0})=({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D},\acute{\mathbf{D}})

such that:

(1)

each
\pi_{i}:{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{i-1}
is a smooth map induced from the blow-up along a suborbifold with complex normal bundle in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i-1}
;

(2)

in the construction of (1), the locus along which each blow-up takes place is a closed substack of
\acute{\mathbf{D}}_{i-1}
;

(3)

for any point
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}_{n}|
and
{\mathcal{D}}\in\acute{\mathbf{D}}_{n}
, the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index (cf. Definition
4.12
) at
x
defined using the pair
({\mathcal{U}}_{n},\mathbf{D}_{n})
is
0
.

If
f:({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})\to({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D},\acute{\mathbf{D}})
defines an open embedding of orbifolds
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
such that
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}=f^{*}\mathbf{D}
and
\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime}=f^{*}\acute{\mathbf{D}}
, then there exists a smooth map
\tilde{f}:(({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})^{dis},(\mathbf{D}^{\prime})^{dis},(\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})^{dis})\to({\mathcal{U}}^{dis},\mathbf{D}^{dis},\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{dis})
inducing an open embedding of orbifolds
\tilde{f}:\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
such that the commutative diagram

(4.31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})^{dis},(\mathbf{D}^{\prime})^{dis},(\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})^{dis})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}}^{dis},\mathbf{D}^{dis},\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{dis})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D},\acute{\mathbf{D}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is Cartesian.

As the reader could guess, before providing a proof of Proposition
4.18
, we need to characterize the loci along which the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index achieves its maximum.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
be a normally complex pair, and suppose
{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathbf{D}
is a divisor. Let
|{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}|\subset|{\mathcal{U}}|
be the subset which consists of points at which the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index achieves its maximum. Then
|{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}|
is a closed subset of
|{\mathcal{U}}|
, and the closed substack
{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
which is defined to be the preimage of
|{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}|
under the coarse space map
{\mathcal{U}}\to|{\mathcal{U}}|
is a suborbifold with complex normal bundle.

Proof.

If all the points in
|{\mathcal{U}}|
have
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index
0
, by definition we have
{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}={\mathcal{U}}
. So let’s assume that there exist one point in
|{\mathcal{U}}|
whose
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index is nonzero.

To prove that
|{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}|
is closed, it suffices to show that the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial function defined over
|{\mathcal{U}}|
is upper semi-continuous. This property is local in nature. Without loss of generality, if the normal invariant of
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
is realized by
(\Gamma,V)
, we can replace the orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
by the global quotient orbifold
({\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V)//\Gamma
where
\Gamma
acts trivially on
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, and
x
corresponds to the image of
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V
in the coarse space. Assuming
k=0
does not affect the argument, so we choose to do so. Using the decomposition (
4.8
), based on the assumption that
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
is everywhere divisorial, we know that

(4.32)

\chi_{div}(x)=\chi(x)=\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle.

Using the same notation, we assume that
{\mathcal{D}}
corresponds to the complex codimension
1
subspace
\{\chi_{i_{j}}=0\}
. It suffices to show that for any other point in
x^{\prime}\in|V//\Gamma|
, its
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index is bounded from above by the quantity (
4.17
). We proceed by assuming that
x^{\prime}
is contained in the image of
|{\mathcal{D}}|
, otherwise the proof is already finished. Suppose that the stabilizer of
x^{\prime}
corresponds to the subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
. Because the divisorial index of
x^{\prime}
is
0
, we know that
\chi(x^{\prime})=\chi_{div}(x^{\prime})
. By definition,
\chi(x^{\prime})\subset\chi(\Gamma^{\prime})
is generated by the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-characters from restricting the
\Gamma
-characters
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{s}
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
, which we denote by
\chi_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}
. The character associated to the normal direction of
{\mathcal{D}}
at
x^{\prime}
is then denoted by
\chi_{i_{j}}^{\prime}
. The restriction of any divisorial character
\chi_{i_{1}},\dots,\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}
of
x
to the group
\Gamma^{\prime}
is trivial if the corresponding divisor does not pass through
x^{\prime}
. As a result, we just need to focus on the contribution of
\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}
to the formula (
4.17
). The group homomorphism (
4.16
) associated to
x^{\prime}
can be written as

(4.33)

\chi(x^{\prime})=\chi_{div}(x^{\prime})\to\chi_{div}(x^{\prime})/\chi_{div}(x^{\prime})^{\mathcal{D}}=\langle\chi_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}\rangle/\langle\chi_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\hat{\chi_{i_{j}}^{\prime}},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}\rangle.

It fits into a commutative diagram

(4.34)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle/\langle\chi_{1},\dots,\hat{\chi_{i_{j}}},\dots,\chi_{s}\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(x^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\chi_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}\rangle/\langle\chi_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\hat{\chi_{i_{j}}^{\prime}},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}\rangle}}

in which the vertical arrows are induced by restricting a
\Gamma
-character to a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-character so that they are surjective. For a character
\chi_{j}^{\prime}\in\{\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}}^{\prime},\dots,\chi_{s}^{\prime}\}
coming from the restriction of the character
\chi_{j}
, because the right vertical arrow takes a generator to a generator and takes the image of
\chi_{j}
to
\chi_{j}^{\prime}
, we know that the contribution of
\chi_{j}^{\prime}
to the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index in exactly
c_{j}
as in (
4.17
) if
\chi_{j}^{\prime}
is nontrivial. Therefore, the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index of
x^{\prime}
is bounded from above by (
4.17
).

To construct the orbifold structure, consider the étale chart
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V
, where the preimage of
|{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}|
corresponds to
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V_{dis}
, in which
V_{dis}
is the direct sum of
\chi_{i_{1}},\dots\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}}}
and
\chi_{j}\in\{\chi_{i_{r^{\prime}+1}},\dots,\chi_{s}\}
such that the image of
\chi_{j}
under the map (
4.16
) is
0
. Accordingly, the finite global quotient orbifold
({\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times V_{dis})//\Gamma
can be used to construct a suborbifold structure on
{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}
. The fiber of the normal bundle
N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}
can be identified with
V/V_{dis}
, which has a natural complex structure.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.18
.

Given a normally complex pair with distinguished structure
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D},\acute{\mathbf{D}})
, if the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index is constantly zero for any
{\mathcal{D}}\in\acute{\mathbf{D}}
, the construction terminates.

Otherwise, we choose
{\mathcal{D}}\in\acute{\mathbf{D}}
with minimal order such that there exists at least a point in
|{\mathcal{U}}|
at which the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index is nonzero. Using
{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}
defined from Lemma
4.19
, we consider the effective and compact normally complex orbifold
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}}{\mathcal{U}}
. It is endowed with divisors
\hat{\mathbf{D}}=\mathbf{D}\cup{\mathbb{P}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{div})
, in which
\mathbf{D}
is the strict transform and the last component denotes the exceptional divisor that is endowed with an order which is greater than all other components from
\mathbf{D}
. The distinguished structure
\acute{\mathbf{D}}
can also be extended to
\acute{\hat{\mathbf{D}}}
by making the exceptional divisor
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathbb{P}}(N_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{U}}_{div})
distinguished. By construction, the ordering constraint in Definition
4.5
is satisfied.

We claim the following holds for the normally complex pair with distinguished structure
(\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}}{\mathcal{U}},\hat{\mathbf{D}},\acute{\hat{\mathbf{D}}})
:

(1)

given any
\hat{x}\in|{\mathcal{F}}|
mapped to
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}|
under the blow-down map, its
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index for any
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
coming from the strict transform of a divisor
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
(here we abuse the notation by using the same symbol for the divisor in the two spaces) in
\mathbf{D}
is at most the
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index of
x
;

(2)

if
{\mathcal{D}}=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
in (1), the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index of
\hat{x}
is strictly smaller than that of
x
;

(3)

for any
\hat{x}\in|{\mathcal{F}}|
, its
{\mathcal{F}}
-divisorial index is strictly smaller than the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index of its image under the blow-down map
x
.

If the claim holds, by ranging over all the divisors in
\acute{\mathbf{D}}
according to the given ordering, we can strictly decrease the maximum of the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index. By the compactness of
{\mathcal{U}}
, the construction terminates after applying the above blow-up construction for finitely many times. The desired functoriality statement holds as the ordering of the
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial indices are preserved by restricting to suborbifolds.

It remains to prove the claim. As in the proof of Lemma
4.19
, we can assume that the orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
is isomorphic to the finite global quotient orbifold
V//\Gamma
, and
V
is decomposed as (
4.8
). Using the notations introduced in the proof of Lemma
4.19
,
{\mathcal{D}}
corresponds to the subspace
\{\chi_{i_{j}}=0\}
, and we can decompose
V
as a direct sum
V_{dis}\oplus V_{dis}^{\perp}
such that
V_{dis}\times\{0\}
defines an étale chart of
{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}
. After applying the blow-up, we can locally identify
\mathrm{Bl}_{{\mathcal{U}}_{dis}}{\mathcal{U}}
with the global quotient
({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}(V_{dis})}(-1)\times V_{dis}^{\perp})//\Gamma=((V_{dis}\setminus\{0\})\times V_{dis}^{\perp})//\Gamma
. We can assume that
\hat{x}
is contained in
|({\mathbb{P}}(V_{dis})\times V_{dis}^{\perp})//\Gamma|
We prove the claims one after another.

(1)

Suppose the coordinate along
\chi_{j}
being
0
specifies the divisor
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
. Because we have presented the blow-up locally as
((V_{dis}\setminus\{0\})\times V_{dis}^{\perp})
, the same argument in the proof of Lemma
4.19
shows that the
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index of
\hat{x}
is bounded from above by
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index of the image of
0\in V
in the coarse space.

(2)

Keep using the notations in (1). In the special case of
{\mathcal{D}}=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
, by assumption we know that
\chi_{j}\subset V_{dis}^{\perp}
, which mean that the image of
\chi_{j}\in\chi(x)
under the map (
4.16
) is nonzero. For the point
\hat{x}
in the blow-up, the image of the character
\chi_{j}\in\chi(\hat{x})
under the map (
4.16
) is necessarily zero. Therefore, the quantity (
4.17
) strictly decreases.

(3)

Now suppose that
\hat{x}\in|{\mathcal{F}}|=|({\mathbb{P}}(V_{dis})\times V_{dis}^{\perp})//\Gamma|
. If
\chi_{j^{\prime}}\subset V_{dis}^{\perp}
, namely
\chi_{j^{\prime}}
is a character which is mapped to a nonzero element under (
4.16
), then it contributes
c_{j^{\prime}}
to (
4.17
). In other words, the image of
\chi_{j^{\prime}}
is
c_{j^{\prime}}
times the generator of
\chi_{\mathcal{D}}(x)
. For the group

(4.35)

\chi_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{x})=\chi_{div}(\hat{x})/\chi_{div}(\hat{x})^{\mathcal{F}}

as defined in (
4.15
), if we view
\hat{x}\in|((V_{dis}\setminus\{0\})\times V_{dis}^{\perp})//\Gamma|
, we see that the image of
\chi_{j^{\prime}}
, which is now thought of as an element in
\chi(\Gamma_{\hat{x}})
after restriction, is actually
c_{j^{\prime}}-1
times the generator of
\chi_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{x})
. So claim (3) is also proven after ranging over all
\chi_{j^{\prime}}\subset V_{dis}^{\perp}
.

∎

4.6.
Proof of Theorem
4.6

Starting from a normally complex pair
({\mathcal{U}},\mathbf{D})
, we first apply Proposition
4.16
to obtain a divisorial normally complex pair
({\mathcal{U}}^{div},\mathbf{D}^{div})
. Then the deorbification is realized by iteratively applying the following four steps.

(1)

Define an invariant for points in
|{\mathcal{U}}^{div}|
to be the lexicographical composition of the independence index (Definition
4.7
), the toroidal index (Definition
4.8
), and the divisorial type (Definition
4.9
). Denote the locus along which such an invariant achieves its maximum by
|{\mathcal{U}}^{div}_{lex}|
.

(2)

The divisorial type is constant along
|{\mathcal{U}}^{div}_{lex}|
by definition. For any
x\in|{\mathcal{U}}^{div}_{lex}|
, its divisorial type
(A(x),\mathbf{v}(x))
defines an orbifold fan
\mathbf{\Sigma}
by Remark
4.15
. Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{0}=\mathbf{\Sigma}
and apply the construction in Proposition
4.14
to obtain a sequence of orbifold fans

(4.36)

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{n}\to\cdots\to\mathbf{\Sigma}_{0}.

(3)

Because each ray in
\Sigma(1)
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is marked by certain index from
\mathbf{D}^{div}
, every star subdivision lifts to the corresponding (possibly) ineffective orbifold chart
V//\Gamma
and can be globalized to the blow-up of
{\mathcal{U}}^{div}
along the corresponding intersection of divisors from
\mathbf{D}^{div}
. Similarly, the root stack construction along toric divisors can be globalized to a root stack construction along the corresponding divisor in the ambient orbifold. As a result, the sequence (
4.36
) can be lifted to a sequence of blow-ups and root constructions of
({\mathcal{U}}^{div},\mathbf{D}^{div})
, whose output is a normally complex pair with distinguished structure
(({\mathcal{U}}^{div})^{\prime},(\mathbf{D}^{div})^{\prime},(\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{div})^{\prime})
.

(4)

Applying Proposition
4.18
to
(({\mathcal{U}}^{div})^{\prime},(\mathbf{D}^{div})^{\prime},(\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{div})^{\prime})
, we obtain a normally complex pair with distinguished structure
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime},\acute{\mathbf{D}}^{\prime\prime})
whose
{\mathcal{D}}
-divisorial index is
0
. Then we forget the distinguished structure to get a normally complex pair
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime})
.

Then we define
(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\tilde{\mathbf{D}})
to be the final output of the above algorithm. The proof that the above lexicographical composition of invariants decreases strictly for each time of the iteration is exactly the same as the argument in
[
Ber17
, Algorithm E]
, because everything can be checked on the toric local models described by orbifold fans. By compactness, we know that the above algorithm must terminate after finitely many steps.

By construction, we see that the independence index of each point in
|\tilde{\mathcal{U}}|
is
0
, which implies that
|\tilde{\mathcal{U}}|
is a smooth manifold. Because all the modifications are functorial under open embeddings, so is the final output. ∎

5.
From orbifolds to manifolds

In this section, we assemble the proofs of all the results stated in the introduction, involving geometric or orbifold bordism.

5.1.
Complex orbifold bordism

If
{\mathcal{U}}
is a orbifold with boundary and
(X,A)
is a pair of topological spaces, we use
f:{\mathcal{U}}\to(X,A)
to denote a continuous map
f:({\mathcal{U}},\partial{\mathcal{U}})\to(X,A)
and express such data as
({\mathcal{U}},f)

over

(X,A)
. The following definition is a convenient way to bypass the need to formulate the notion of (derived) orbifold with corners:

Definition 5.1
.

A pair
({\mathcal{U}},f)
and
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
of orbifolds over
(X,A)
, are
bordant
if there exist an orbifold with boundary
\hat{\mathcal{U}}
and a continuous map
F:\hat{\mathcal{U}}\to X
such that:

(1)

there exists a codimension
0
embedding
{\mathcal{U}}\coprod{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to\partial\hat{\mathcal{U}}
along which
F
restricts to
f\coprod f^{\prime}
;

(2)

the map
F
restricts a continuous map of pairs
(\hat{\mathcal{U}},\partial\hat{\mathcal{U}}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}^{\circ}\coprod({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})^{\circ})\to(X,A)
.

The
orbifold bordism group
of
(X,A)
is then defined to be the free abelian group generated by isomorphism classes of
({\mathcal{U}},f)
, with
{\mathcal{U}}
compact, over
(X,A)
modulo the equivalence relations generated by bordism. It is straightforward to see that this is a homology theory, with boundary homomorphism given by restricting
f
to the boundary of
\mathcal{U}
.

One can impose tangential structures on
{\mathcal{U}}
to define
oriented
or
framed
orbifold bordism groups. We focus our attention on the following case:

Definition 5.2
.

A
stable complex structure
on an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
(with boundary) consists of a complex a complex structure on
T{\mathcal{U}}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, for some integer
k
.

The boundary of any stably complex orbifold admits a natural stable complex structure, given by identifying the choice of normal vector with an additional
{\mathbb{R}}
stabilization factor. Then we say that any stably complex pair
({\mathcal{U}},f)
and
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
over
(X,A)
are stably complex bordant if there exists a bordism
(\hat{\mathcal{U}},F)
as above further so that

•

the stable complex structure on
\hat{\mathcal{U}}
restricts to the stable complex structures of
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
for some choice of normal vector field.

Definition 5.3
.

The
complex orbifold bordism
group of a pair of topological spaces
(X,A)

(5.1)

\Omega_{*}^{U}(X,A)

is the abelian group (with additive structure coming from disjoint union) generated by compact orbifolds with boundary over
(X,A)
, equipped with a stable complex structure, modulo the equivalence relation generated by stably complex bordisms. It is
graded
by the (virtual) dimension.

5.2.
Constructing manifolds from orbifolds

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.20
